# Diagram and Writing Five

->**The Diagram of the Intersecting Numbers of Heaven and Earth according to the Luo River Writing**<-
<note>original: "洛書" (Luo Shu). Ancient Chinese cosmology describes this diagram as appearing on the back of a mystical tortoise from the Luo River. Unlike the "River Chart," which focuses on the birth of elements, the Luo Shu represents the dynamic balance and the "ordered change" of the world in its manifest state.</note>

<image-desc>
A cosmological "Magic Square" known as the Luo Shu. It is composed of white circles (Yang, odd numbers) and black circles (Yin, even numbers) arranged in a 3x3 grid:
- **Top (South):** 9 white circles
- **Bottom (North):** 1 white circle
- **Left (East):** 3 white circles
- **Right (West):** 7 white circles
- **Center:** 5 white circles
- **Top-Left (Southeast):** 4 black circles
- **Top-Right (Southwest):** 2 black circles
- **Bottom-Left (Northeast):** 8 black circles
- **Bottom-Right (Northwest):** 6 black circles
All rows, columns, and diagonals sum to fifteen.
</image-desc>

Yang Ziyun <note>Yang Xiong (53 BCE – 18 CE), a celebrated philosopher of the Han Dynasty who synthesized divination and mathematics.</note> said: "1 and 6 share the same lineage; 2 and 7 are peers; 3 and 8 are companions; 4 and 9 follow the same path; 5 and 10 <note>The original text uses a symbolic placeholder for 10, representing the hidden completion of the central five.</note> guard one another." This statement refers precisely to the numerical relationships shown in this diagram.

Master Zhu <note>Zhu Xi (1130–1200), the preeminent Neo-Confucian who standardized the interpretation of these diagrams for centuries.</note> remarked that by analyzing the combinations of the numbers 6, 7, 8, and 9 <note>These are the "Old Yin," "Young Yang," "Young Yin," and "Old Yang" numbers used in I Ching divination.</note>, we can derive the primary trigrams **Heaven** (Qian), **Earth** (Kun), **Water** (Kan), and **Fire** (Li). These then occupy the <term>four cardinal positions</term> <note>North, South, East, and West.</note>. 

Furthermore, by following the sequence of the numbers 1, 2, 3, and 4, we form the remaining trigrams **Mountain** (Gen), **Lake** (Dui), **Thunder** (Zhen), and **Wind** (Xun), which fill the gaps in the <term>four corners</term> <note>The intermediate directions: Northeast, Southeast, Northwest, and Southwest.</note>. This arrangement aligns perfectly with the numerical grid of the Luo Shu.

By examining these symbols of "generation" (the birth of forces) and "completion" (their final form), and observing their division and union, their progression and recession, one can understand the underlying rhythm of the universe.

## To the right is the image of the Luo River Writing, with horizontal and vertical sums of fifteen.

->**The Nine Categories of the Great Plan**<-
<note>The following text summarizes the "Great Plan" (Hong Fan), a foundational text on Chinese governance and cosmology found in the *Classic of History*.</note>

The *Great Plan* states: First is the **Five Elements**; second is the **Reverent Practice of the Five Matters**; third is the **Earnest Devotion to the Eight Regulated Affairs of Government**; fourth is the **Harmonious Use of the Five Regulators of Time**; fifth is the **Establishment of the Royal Perfection**; sixth is the **Orderly Use of the Three Virtues**; seventh is the **Lucid Application of the Examination of Doubts**; eighth is the **Thoughtful Use of the Various Omens**; ninth is the **Urging Toward the Five Blessings and Awe-inspiring Use of the Six Extremities**. 

<note>original: "五行... 五事... 八政... 五紀... 皇極... 三德... 稽疑... 庶徵... 五福... 六極"</note>

Scholars of the Han Dynasty <note>roughly 206 BCE – 220 CE</note> considered these sixty-five characters to be the original text of the *Luo River Writing*. However, the tradition passed down by Master Xiyi <note>Chen Tuan (c. 871–989 CE), a famous Taoist sage who was influential in the development of Neo-Confucian cosmology</note> holds that this is the "Third Transformation of the Dragon Diagram," and takes the "Generative Diagram" to be the original text of the *Luo River Writing*. 

This discrepancy likely arose from errors made during the transcription and copying of the texts, which sparked the long-standing debate over whether the "Diagram" <note>the River Chart</note> should be based on the number nine or ten, and whether the "Writing" <note>the Luo River Writing</note> should be based on the number ten or nine. 

If we were to simply swap the names of these two images today, the relationship between the Dragon Diagram <note>River Chart</note> and the Tortoise Writing <note>Luo River Writing</note> would become clear and self-evident, requiring no further debate.

<summary>This page discusses the historical interpretation of the "Luo River Writing" (Luo Shu) and its connection to the Nine Categories of the "Great Plan." It notes the scholarly debate regarding the correct numbering and names of the two primary cosmological diagrams.</summary>

<keywords>Luo Shu, Great Plan, Hong Fan, Five Elements, Chen Tuan, Han Dynasty scholars, River Chart, cosmology</keywords>

The *Great Commentary on the Changes* <note>also known as the *Xici* or the *Appended Phrases*</note> says: "The Yellow River brought forth the Chart, and the Luo River brought forth the Writing; the Sages followed them as a pattern."

Kong Anguo <note>a prominent scholar of the Han Dynasty and descendant of Confucius</note> says: "As for the **River Chart**, it appeared when Fuxi <note>the legendary first sovereign of China</note> ruled the world. A dragon-horse emerged from the River, and Fuxi modeled its markings to draw the **Eight Trigrams**. As for the **Luo Writing**, it appeared when Yu the Great <note>the legendary founder of the Xia Dynasty</note> was taming the floods. A divine turtle carried markings on its back, arranged in numbers up to nine. Yu then followed their sequence to establish the **Nine Categories** <note>original: "九類", referring to the 'Great Plan' for governing the world</note>." 

Liu Xin <note>an influential astronomer and librarian of the Han Dynasty, c. 50 BCE – 23 CE</note> says: "Fuxi succeeded Heaven as King and received the **River Chart**, using it as a pattern to draw the **Eight Trigrams**. When Yu the Great tamed the floods, he was granted the laws of the **Luo Writing**, which he arranged into the **Nine Categories**. The River Chart and the Luo Writing serve as the warp and woof <note>the vertical and horizontal threads of a weave</note> for one another, just as the Eight Trigrams and the Nine Chapters reflect one another as internal and external versions of the same truth."

Guan Ziming <note>also known as Guan Lang, a philosopher of the Sui/Tang era</note> says: "The patterns of the **River Chart** consist of seven in the front, six in the back, eight on the left, and nine on the right. The patterns of the **Luo Writing**..."

# Illustrated Explanation of the Book of Changes Imagery: Inner Volume

[...is] thirty. In total, the **numbers of Heaven and Earth** <term>Tiandi zhi shu (天地之數): the sum of the first ten integers (1-10), representing the complete numerical basis of the universe</term> sum to fifty-five. This is how transformations are completed and the **spirits** <note>original: "鬼神" (guishen); in this context, it refers to the expansion and contraction of natural forces or spiritual energies rather than literal ghosts.</note> are set in motion.

This section serves to elucidate the numbers found within the **Chart and the Writing** <note>Referring to the River Chart (Hetu) and the Luo Writing (Luoshu).</note>. Generally, every odd number is **Yang**; Yang represents the numbers of Heaven. Every even number is **Yin**; Yin represents the numbers of Earth. The numbers of Heaven are 1, 3, 5, 7, and 9; their sum is twenty-five. The numbers of Earth are 2, 4, 6, 8, and 10; their sum is thirty. Combining these two sums results in fifty-five. The transformations of Heaven and Earth and the expansion and contraction of Yin and Yang never exceed these numbers.

From these numbers:
*   By pairing them, we find the **Two Polarities** <term>Er Yi (二儀): Yin and Yang</term>.
*   By tripling them, we find the **Three Powers** <term>San Cai (三才): Heaven, Earth, and Humanity</term>.
*   By grouping them in fives, we find the **Five Elements** <term>Wu Xing (五行): Wood, Fire, Earth, Metal, and Water</term>.
*   By dividing them, we find the **Eight Trigrams** <term>Ba Gua (八卦): the foundational symbols of the I Ching representing natural forces</term>.
*   By investigating them to the end, we find the **Nine Palaces** <term>Jiu Gong (九宮): the nine sectors of the magic square used in cosmic mapping and divination</term>.

This is the great essential of the system. **Master Zhu** <note>original: "朱子", referring to Zhu Xi (1130–1200 CE), the preeminent Neo-Confucian scholar who synthesized these numerical theories</note> says: "Between Heaven and Earth, there is only **one breath** <term>yi qi (一氣): the primordial vital energy or substance of the universe</term>..."

# Imperial Edition of the Complete Library of the Four Treasuries

## Illustrated Explanations of the Imagery of the Changes: Inner Chapters

...those that reside between the numbers six, seven, eight, and nine are these. “Grouping by fives” <term>Wu (伍): originally a military term for a squad of five men</term> refers to organizing the numbers into five distinct positions, much like the arrangement of military ranks into units of tens and fives. 

Regarding the grouping of these fives: if we examine the transformations within the diagrams, it is as if the numbers one, two, three, and four are grouped atop the numbers six, seven, eight, and nine. 

“Interweaving” <term>Cuo (錯): the lateral or horizontal crossing of positions</term> refers to crossing and interchanging elements, placing one on the left and one on the right. Examining the transformations in the diagrams, this is demonstrated by the numbers three and four occupying the left and right positions interchangeably. 

“Synthesizing” <term>Zong (綜): the vertical inversion or "weaving together" of elements</term> means to gather and lift them, referring to the relationship between low and high. Examining the transformations in the diagrams, this is seen where one and two occupy the upper and lower positions, alternating between being low and being high. 

Having already varied the numbers by threes and grouped them by fives through these transformations—interweaving and interchanging them, synthesizing and crossing them—the beautiful patterns of Heaven and Earth are fully formed, and the archetypal imagery of all things under Heaven is determined. Is it not true, then, that the **River Chart** and the **Luo Writing** are the very origins of the ultimate transformations in the world?

# In ancient times, when the Sages created the **Book of Changes**, they sought mysterious aid from the divine luminaries and produced the milfoil stalks <note>original: "蓍" (shī); yarrow stalks used in ancient Chinese divination to generate the numbers of a hexagram.</note>. Taking three as the number for Heaven and two for Earth...

-># Volume 1<-

**Relying on numbers, they observed the transformations of Yin and Yang to establish the hexagrams; giving full expression to the Firm and the Yielding, they generated the hexagram lines; harmonizing and according with the Way and its Virtue, they ordered all things by what is right; and by exhausting the Principle and fulfilling one’s Nature, they reached the realization of the Mandate** <note>original: "命" (mìng), referring to the divine decree or the cosmic destiny of a person's life</note>.

This chapter expresses the great purpose of the Sages when they created the *Classic of Changes* <term>Yi Jing: the foundational text of Chinese divination and philosophy</term> and serves as the original source for the numbers derived from yarrow stalks and the structure of the hexagram lines. 

The phrase "providing secret aid to the divine intelligences" means that the Sages practiced ritual fasting and "cleansed their hearts" <note>a metaphor for purifying the mind of all worldly distractions</note> to withdraw into a state of profound, inward secrecy. In this state, the divine intelligences secretly assisted and silently protected their work. 

The forty-nine yarrow stalks <term>Shi (蓍): stalks of the yarrow plant used in ancient divination</term> are used through the technical process of dividing, hanging, and counting <term>She Gui (揲歸): the specific mathematical manipulation of stalks to determine a hexagram line</term> to generate the numerical measures of Yin, Yang, the Firm, and the Yielding. 

The numbers of Heaven and the numbers of Earth rely on the relationship of "threes and twos" <note>original: "參兩" (sān liǎng), a mathematical ratio used to derive the values of the lines</note> to clarify the imagery of the numbers nine, eight, seven, and six <note>In I Ching divination, 9 and 7 represent Yang, while 8 and 6 represent Yin</note>. 

Therefore, by observing the transformations of the Yin and Yang of Heaven, the archetypal images of the hexagrams were established. By giving full expression to the Firm and the Yielding of the Earth, the specific meanings of the individual lines were born. By harmonizing with the Way and its Virtue, every principle and pattern found its proper place, thereby exhausting the fundamental principles of Heaven and Earth, Yin and Yang, and the Firm and the Yielding.

By fully realizing one’s own innate nature <note>original: "性" (xìng), the internal character or essence of a being</note> to the point of fully realizing the nature of all people and things, one can assist in the transforming and nourishing work of Heaven and Earth. In doing so, one exists without any separation from the constant flow of creation. This is the ultimate achievement of the Sage's supreme sincerity.

In ancient times, when the Sages created the *Changes*, they intended to align it with the principles of Nature and Destiny <term>Xingming (性命): the relationship between an individual’s innate character and their cosmic purpose or life span</term>. Because of this, they established the Way of Heaven, calling it Yin and Yang; they established the Way of Earth, calling it the Yielding and the Firm; and they established the Way of Humanity, calling it Benevolence and Righteousness. By combining these Three Powers <term>Sancai (三才): the three components of the universe—Heaven, Earth, and Humanity</term> and doubling them, the *Changes* formed a hexagram through six lines. By distinguishing between Yin and Yang and alternating the use of the Yielding and the Firm, the *Changes* formed a pattern through six positions.

When the Sages created the *Changes* to accord with the principles of Nature and Destiny, they did so primarily for the sake of humanity. Therefore, they observed Yin and Yang to establish the Way of Heaven—the Way of Heaven being nothing more than the cycles of cold and heat, or day and night. They examined...

# Imperially Commissioned Complete Library of the Four Treasuries, Volume One

...based on the Firm and the Yielding <note>original: "剛柔" (gāng róu), the earthly counterparts to the heavenly Yin and Yang</note>, they established the Way of Earth. The Way of Earth is simply the flowing [of waters], the towering [of mountains], the birthing [of life], and the growing [of plants]. Rooted in Benevolence and Righteousness, they established the Way of Humanity. The Way of Humanity is simply filial piety, fraternal respect, loyalty, and trustworthiness <term>Xiao, Ti, Zhong, Xin: the four fundamental Confucian virtues governing family and social relations</term>.

For humans receive the vital energy <term>Qi (氣): the life force or breath that animates the universe</term> of Yin and Yang to have life; they are endowed with the substance of the Firm and the Yielding to have physical form; and they possess the principles of Benevolence and Righteousness to complete their innate nature. When vital energy, physical form, and substance are all present, and the nature is fulfilled, then the Way of the Three Powers <term>Sancai (三才): the cosmic trinity of Heaven, Earth, and Humanity</term> is perfected.

Therefore, in terms of the eight trigrams <note>the three-line symbols that form the basis of the Changes</note>, the bottom line represents Earth, the middle line represents Humanity, and the top line represents Heaven; they are distinguished by the difference between odd and even lines <note>solid lines are "odd/Yang" and broken lines are "even/Yin"</note>. By combining these Three Powers and doubling them to form the "doubled hexagrams" <note>the sixty-four six-line figures</note>, the first and second lines represent Earth, the third and fourth lines represent Humanity, and the fifth and top lines represent Heaven.

Speaking of them individually, the first, third, and fifth lines are the "Yang positions," while the second, fourth, and top lines are the "Yin positions." Yang constitutes the Firm, and Yin constitutes the Yielding. Yin, Yang, the Firm, and the Yielding are used alternately among the six lines of a single hexagram, interweaving and—

# Imperially Commissioned Complete Library of the Four Treasuries / Illustrations and Explanations of the Images of the Changes: Inner Chapter

### Fifteen

## The Formation of the Patterns

The *Classic of Changes* <term>Yi (易): also known as the I Ching</term> as a book is vast and all-encompassing. Within it, there is the Way of Heaven, the Way of Humanity, and the Way of Earth. It takes these Three Powers <term>Sancai (三才): the cosmic trinity of Heaven, Earth, and Humanity</term> and doubles them; therefore, there are six lines. These six lines are nothing other than the Way of the Three Powers. Because this Way is characterized by constant change and movement, the lines are called **Yao** <term>Yao (爻): the individual horizontal lines, either solid (Yang) or broken (Yin), that compose a hexagram</term>. Because these lines occupy different levels of status, they are called **entities**. Because these entities intermingle, they are called **patterns**. When these patterns are not properly aligned, good and bad fortune arise.

The Way mentioned here is the Way of Heaven, Earth, and Humanity. The interaction of the heavenly "One Yin and One Yang" forms the lines; the interaction of the earthly "One Yielding and One Firm" forms the lines. When the six positions are established, they form a coherent order <note>original: "文章" (wénzhāng), which here refers to a beautiful, patterned arrangement or "tapestry" of the cosmos rather than a written "essay"</note>; this is what is meant by "change and movement." The lines are those things which imitate this process. The lines are those things which imitate the movements of all things under heaven. "Hierarchy" <note>original: "等" (děng)</note> refers to the degrees of difference and rank between the lines. <gloss>the character "hierarchy"</gloss>

<summary>This page explains why hexagrams consist of six lines, linking the structure to the doubling of the Three Powers (Heaven, Earth, and Humanity). It defines the individual lines (Yao) as imitations of universal movement and the basis for cosmic patterns.</summary>

<keywords>Three Powers, Yao, hexagram structure, cosmic order, change and movement, Siku Quanshu, I Ching philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 46 ---
<meta>This page continues the detailed exploration of hexagram structure. While the previous page introduced the concept of the six lines (Yao) representing the Three Powers (Heaven, Earth, and Humanity), this page narrows the focus to the specific rankings of those lines, the numerical values associated with them, and how these elements combine to form the "patterns" or "tapestry" of the cosmos.</meta>

# Volume One

The character for **ranks** <note>original: "等" (děng), meaning levels, classes, or ranks</note> is derived from the character for "bamboo" and "calculation" <term>Suan (筭): an ancient bamboo counting rod</term>. It is composed of the characters for "ten" and "one," representing the end and the beginning of numbers. It refers to the act of drawing out, holding, and arranging the lines into ranks; this is the intended meaning of the character. Older explanations suggested that "ranks" was derived from "temple," but this lacks meaningful connection to the concept; therefore, I am correcting it here.

Within the states of movement and stillness, there are the ranks of the First, Second, Third, Fourth, Fifth, and Top positions. There are also the numerical variations of Seven, Eight, Nine, and Six. Therefore, these are called **entities** <note>original: "物" (wù), meaning objects or things; in this context, it refers to the lines as representations of specific cosmic entities</note>.

These entities are entities of time. Seven and Nine are odd numbers; all entities that are Yang in nature and firm in substance are considered **Entities of Qian** <term>Qian (乾): The Creative, representing Heaven and pure Yang energy</term>. Six and Eight are even numbers; all entities that are Yin in nature and yielding in substance are considered **Entities of Kun** <term>Kun (坤): The Receptive, representing Earth and pure Yin energy</term>. <gloss>When analyzed separately, Nine is Yang and Six is Yin, while Seven is Firm and Eight is Yielding. Yin and Yang are "images" <note>abstract cosmic principles</note>, whereas Firm and Yielding are "forms" <note>the physical manifestation of those principles</note>. Therefore, Nine and Six are subject to change, while Seven and Eight remain constant. The lines use Nine and Six because they primarily deal with change.</gloss>

<gloss>Furthermore, it is said that the number for elders is Seven and the number for the hexagrams is Eight. The physical substance of the Firm and Yielding is the means by which Yin and Yang act. When their virtues combine, Qian is established. The lines of Qian use Nine, and the lines of Kun use Six; this is the application of Yin and Yang.</gloss>

The Firm and Yielding possess substance; they intermingle and cross one another to form a "tapestry" <term>Wenzhang (文章): literally "patterns and brilliance," referring here to the beautiful, orderly arrangement of the universe</term>. <gloss>The character for "pattern" (文) is derived from "to cross" (乂), which is the image of Yin and Yang intersecting.</gloss>

When these patterns are appropriate and aligned, then there is **Auspiciousness**. When one's position is correct and one's movements are balanced and centered, they accord with what is appropriate for all entities, and thus good fortune arises.

It defines the cosmic "tapestry" as the intersection of Yin and Yang, which leads to balance and good fortune.</summary>

**Page 16**
**1**

...indeed. The character for **auspicious** <note>original: "吉" (jí), meaning good fortune or lucky</note> is composed of the character for "ten" <note>十 (shí)</note>, which represents Yin transforming into Yang, and the character for "mouth" <note>口 (kǒu)</note>, which represents Yang transforming into Yin. The character for "one" <note>一 (yī)</note> acts as a phonetic element <term>Xiesheng (諧聲): a category of Chinese characters where one part suggests the sound</term> residing in the midst of change. When change is able to achieve correctness, it is considered "auspicious." As a compound ideograph <term>Huiyi (會意): a character whose meaning is derived from the combination of its parts</term>, the *Book of Documents* says: "Virtue is singular and unified; in movement there is nothing that is not auspicious." The *Commentaries* state: "The movements of the world are made constant by the One."

## When the patterns are improper, misfortune follows

Wang Luzhai of Jinhua <note>Wang Bo (1227–1273), a prominent scholar of the Yuan dynasty known for his deep study of the Book of Changes</note> regarded the character for "auspicious" as an image of "Old Yang" <term>Lao Yang: the peak of active, masculine energy</term> emerging from the earth. 

However, if one's position loses its "center" and one's movement is not "correct," then one falls into the "Chasm" and misfortune is born. <gloss>The character for **misfortune** <note>original: "凶" (xiōng)</note> is derived from the "X" shape <note>乂 (yì)</note> which represents change and movement</gloss>. When movement is trapped within a "mouth," one loses their central correctness and meets misfortune; here, the "mouth" represents a pit or a trap. As a compound ideograph, Wang Luzhai of Jinhua regarded the character for "misfortune" as an image of "Old Yin" <term>Lao Yin: the peak of receptive, feminine energy</term> entering into the earth. Therefore:

# It is said: Good fortune, misfortune, regret, and humiliation arise from movement

Master Zhou said: "The Great Ultimate moves and produces Yang; when movement reaches its limit, it becomes still. In stillness, it produces Yin; when stillness reaches its limit, it moves again. One movement and one stillness act as each other's root. Once Yin and Yang are divided, the Two Primary Forms are established."

He modeled them after Earth <insert>⚋</insert> <gloss>represented by a dot</gloss>.

The <insert>⚋</insert> is named the "Even" and is called <term>Yin</term>.
<note>Yang is represented by a solid line, signifying unity and strength, while Yin is a broken line, signifying receptivity and multiplicity.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates the transition from the undifferentiated Great Ultimate into the Two Primary Forms (Yin and Yang), further expanding into the Four Images and Five Elements. It explains the mythological and philosophical origin of the trigram strokes as being modeled after the energies of Heaven and Earth.</summary>

# To the Right: The Imagery of the Two Primary Forms Producing the Four Images
<gloss>The *Book of Changes* originally had no <term>Eight Trigrams</term>; there were only these principles of <term>Heaven and Earth</term>.</gloss>
<note>original: "易本无八卦止有此乾坤" — The "Eight Trigrams" (Bagua) are the eight basic symbols used in divination. "Heaven and Earth" here refers to the hexagrams Qian and Kun, representing the absolute masculine and feminine principles.</note>

The scholar <term>Master Zhu</term> says: The <term>Yang Form</term> generates an odd line to become <term>Greater Yang</term>, and an even line to become <term>Lesser Yin</term>. The <term>Yin Form</term> generates an odd line to become <term>Lesser Yang</term>, and an even line to become <term>Greater Yin</term>.
<note>Master Zhu refers to Zhu Xi (1130–1200), the most influential Neo-Confucian philosopher. He is explaining how the solid (odd) and broken (even) lines of the I Ching combine to form the four basic two-line symbols.</note>

Older diagrams showed the <term>Four Images</term> laid out flat in a sequence of endless generation. The current diagram shows the <term>Yang Form</term> below, producing one odd and one even line to represent <term>Yin and Yang</term>, while the <term>Yin Form</term> above produces one odd and one even line to represent <term>Hardness and Softness</term>.
<note>"Hardness and Softness" (gangrou) are the earthly manifestations of the cosmic Yin and Yang energies.</note>

The Four Images rotate in a circular motion, cycling without end. Hardness intersects with Yin, and Yin intersects with Hardness; Yang intersects with Softness, and Softness intersects with Yang. By intersecting above and below, left and right, the <term>myriad things</term> are born from them.
<note>The "myriad things" (wanwu) is a traditional Chinese term for all of creation and every distinct object in the universe.</note>

<term>Master Zhou</term> says: Through the transformation of Yang and the union of Yin, the <term>Five Energies</term>—Water, Fire, Wood, Metal, and Earth—are produced. These energies are distributed in their proper order, and the four seasons proceed accordingly.
<note>Master Zhou refers to Zhou Dunyi (1017–1073). His "Five Energies" (Wuqi) are more commonly known in the West as the Five Elements or Five Phases.</note>

As the <term>Commentary</term> says: "Establishing the Way of Heaven is called Yin and Yang; establishing the Way of Earth is called Softness and Hardness." This is exactly what is meant here.
<note>This quote comes from the "Shuo Gua," one of the "Ten Wings" or ancient commentaries on the *Book of Changes* (I Ching).</note>

<summary>This page explains the transition from the two primary forces (Yin and Yang) into the Four Images and Five Elements. It emphasizes the cyclical, intersecting nature of these forces as the source of all life and the seasonal cycle.</summary>
<keywords>Four Images, Zhu Xi, Five Elements, Zhou Dunyi, Yin and Yang, Hardness and Softness, I Ching, Neo-Confucianism</keywords>

To the right, the <term>Two Primary Forms</term> produce the <term>Four Images</term>. The <term>Yin Form</term> produces an odd line to become <term>Lesser Yang</term>. <term>Lesser Yang</term> represents the Yang energy within Yin. 

It is the direction of the East. Its Yang energy generates all living things, and in the cycle of the seasons, it is Spring. Spring means "to stir"; as things stir into life, they become capable of movement. 
<note>original: "春蠢也" — The text uses a wordplay where "Spring" (Chun 春) is linked to the word "to stir" or "to wriggle" (Chun 蠢), suggesting the first movements of insects and plants in the new year.</note>

Therefore, it corresponds to the <term>Compass</term>. 
<note>The "Compass" (Gui) is one of the five standards of measurement, here representing the perfect circularity and budding potential of the Wood element.</note>

In Heaven, it is Wind; on Earth, it is Wood; above, it is the <term>Year Star</term>. 
<note>The "Year Star" (Sui Xing) is the planet Jupiter.</note>

In virtue, it is <term>Primacy</term>; Primacy is the chief of all goodness. 
<note>"Primacy" (Yuan) is the first of the four virtues of the I Ching, representing the creative beginning of all things.</note>

In the body, it is the sinews; in the organs, it is the liver; it opens into the eyes. In the emotions, it is anger; its sound is a shout; its color is deep azure; its taste is sour; its musical note is <term>Jue</term>; its animal is the chicken; its grain is wheat. 

Its number is three. <gloss>The generative number of Wood.</gloss>

The <term>Yang Form</term> below produces an odd line to become <term>Greater Yang</term>. <term>Greater Yang</term> is the Yang energy within Yang. 

It is the direction of the South. Its Yang energy nourishes all living things, and in the cycle of the seasons, it is Summer. Summer means "to grow large"; as things reach their full size, they become expanded and level. 
<note>original: "夏假也" — The text explains "Summer" (Xia 夏) through a homophone "Jia" (假), which in ancient contexts meant "great" or "to reach," signifying the peak of growth.</note>

Therefore, it corresponds to the <term>Balance</term>. 
<note>The "Balance" (Heng) refers to a weighing scale, representing the peak of the sun's path where energy is balanced and fully manifest.</note>

## Middle Scroll

Its virtue is **Sincerity** <gloss>Master Zhou says: "Supreme success" is the penetration of sincerity; "furthering perseverance" is the return of sincerity.</gloss>
<note>original: "周子曰元亨誠之通利貞誠之復" — Master Zhou Dunyi (1017–1073), a foundational Neo-Confucian philosopher, interprets the four virtues of the I Ching (Yuan, Heng, Li, Zhen) as the movement and stillness of "Sincerity" (Cheng), the ultimate reality of the universe.</note>

In the body, it is the flesh; in the internal organs, it is the spleen; it opens into the mouth. In the mind's intent, it is thought; its sound is singing; its color is yellow; its taste is sweet; its musical note is <term>Gong</term> <note>The first and central note of the Chinese pentatonic scale, traditionally associated with the element of Earth and the position of the Sovereign.</note>. 

Its domestic animal is the ox; its grain is millet; its number is five <gloss>the generating number of Earth</gloss>.
<note>In the "River Map" (Hetu) cosmology, numbers are assigned to elements. "Generating numbers" (1 through 5) represent the birth of an element, while "completed numbers" (6 through 10) represent its realization. Five is the generating number for Earth, situated at the center.</note>

# The Eight Trigrams Arranged in Order

->**Heaven**<-
<note>original: "天" (Tian). Here representing the celestial source or the "Upper" realm of pure energy.</note>

/ \
**Yang** <column-break/> **Yin**
<note>The primary duality of the universe. Yang represents light, activity, and the male principle; Yin represents darkness, stillness, and the female principle.</note>

| | | |
**Qian 1** **Dui 2** **Li 3** **Zhen 4**
<note>The first four trigrams are generated from the celestial movement. 1. **Qian** (The Creative/Heaven), 2. **Dui** (The Joyous/Lake), 3. **Li** (The Clinging/Fire), 4. **Zhen** (The Arousing/Thunder).</note>

\ | /
->**The Great Enterprise**<-
<note>original: "大業" (Da Ye). A term from the *Appended Explanations* (Xici) of the I Ching. It refers to the magnificent, ongoing process of universal creation and the fulfillment of one's nature within it.</note>
/ | \

**Xun 5** **Kan 6** **Gen 7** **Kun 8**
<note>The final four trigrams are generated from the terrestrial base. 5. **Xun** (The Gentle/Wind), 6. **Kan** (The Abysmal/Water), 7. **Gen** (Keeping Still/Mountain), 8. **Kun** (The Receptive/Earth).</note>

| | | |
**Firmness** <column-break/> **Yielding**
<note>original: "剛" (Gang) and "柔" (Rou). These terms are the terrestrial equivalents of Yang and Yin, describing the physical qualities of "hard" and "soft" matter.</note>
\ /

->**Earth**<-
<note>original: "地" (Di). Representing the terrestrial foundation or the "Lower" realm of physical form.</note>

***

### Explanation of the Diagram
This diagram visualizes the "Prior Heaven" logic of how the Eight Trigrams are structured. It shows that the universe is not a chaotic collection of symbols, but a structured hierarchy. The top half descends from **Heaven** through the forces of **Yang and Yin**, while the bottom half ascends from **Earth** through the qualities of **Firmness and Yielding**. 

All these forces meet in the center at the **Great Enterprise**, suggesting that the purpose of the I Ching’s transformations is the actualization of life and cosmic order. The numbering (one through eight) follows the "Shao Yong" sequence, a famous method of binary progression used by Song Dynasty philosophers to explain the birth of the universe.

<summary>This page presents a cosmological diagram showing the Eight Trigrams branching out from Heaven and Earth and converging in the "Great Enterprise," representing the orderly manifestation of the universe.</summary>
<keywords>Eight Trigrams, Bagua, Heaven and Earth, Great Enterprise, Yang and Yin, Firmness and Yielding, Shao Yong sequence, Neo-Confucian cosmology</keywords>

### Imperial Edition of the Complete Library in Four Sections, Middle Volume

To the right is the image of the Four Images producing the Eight Trigrams. <gloss>The I Ching originally did not have the sixty-four hexagrams; it only had these eight trigrams.</gloss>

In ancient times, when the sovereign <term>Baoxi</term><note>original: "包犧氏" (Baoxi Shi). Also known as Fuxi, the legendary first emperor and culture hero of China, credited with the invention of writing, fishing, and the Eight Trigrams.</note> ruled over the world, he looked up and observed the images in the heavens, and he looked down and observed the patterns on the earth. He observed the markings of birds and beasts and the various suitabilities of the land <gloss>Between the words "and" and "earth," one version of the text includes the word "heaven."</gloss>. Near at hand, he took inspiration from his own body; farther away, he took inspiration from things in general. Thereupon, he first created the <term>Eight Trigrams</term><note>original: "八卦" (Bagua). Eight symbols consisting of three lines each, representing the fundamental principles of reality (Heaven, Earth, Thunder, Wind, Water, Fire, Mountain, Lake).</note> in order to communicate with the virtue of the divine and the bright, and to classify the essential natures of the myriad things.

To the right is the explanation of the reasons why the <term>Sage</term><note>Refers here to Baoxi/Fuxi.</note> created the trigrams.

In the *I Ching*, there is the <term>Great Ultimate</term><note>original: "太極" (Taiji). The supreme, undifferentiated source of all existence from which all things originate.</note>. This produces the <term>Two Polarities</term><note>original: "兩儀" (Liangyi). Yin and Yang.</note>. The two polarities produce the <term>Four Images</term><note>original: "四象" (Sixiang). Old Yang, Young Yin, Young Yang, and Old Yin; the intermediate stage between polarity and the complexity of the trigrams.</note>, and the four images produce the Eight Trigrams. The eight trigrams determine good and ill fortune, and good and ill fortune generate the <term>Great Enterprise</term>.

To the right is the explanation of the purpose for which the Sage drew the trigrams.

<summary>This page records the legendary origin of the Eight Trigrams by the Sage Fuxi, explaining how he derived these symbols from observing nature to harmonize human life with the divine. It also details the cosmological progression from the singular Great Ultimate (Taiji) to the diverse "Great Enterprise" of the universe.</summary>
<keywords>Baoxi (Fuxi), Eight Trigrams, Great Ultimate (Taiji), Two Polarities, Four Images, Sage, I Ching, Great Enterprise, divination, cosmology</keywords>

--- Page 59 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical and cosmological explanation of the Eight Trigrams, moving from their legendary creation by Fuxi to the specific mechanics of how they are formed and arranged in the "Pre-heaven" (Primordial) sequence. It builds on the previous page's introduction of Fuxi by detailing the observations of Heaven and Earth that led to the specific symbols.</meta>

To the right are the <term>Four Images</term><note>original: "四象" (Si Xiang). In I Ching cosmology, these are the four possible combinations of two Yin or Yang lines (Great Yang, Lesser Yang, Great Yin, Lesser Yin), representing the next stage of creation after the two primary forces.</note> where Yang descends to interact with the Yielding, and the Yielding ascends to interact with the Yang, thereby forming <term>Qian</term><note>The Creative (Heaven)</note> and <term>Kun</term><note>The Receptive (Earth)</note> <gloss>Heaven and Earth take their positions</gloss>, and <term>Gen</term><note>Keeping Still (Mountain)</note> and <term>Dui</term><note>The Joyous (Lake)</note> <gloss>Mountain and Lake resonate with vital energy</gloss>. The Firm ascends to interact with the Yin, and Yin descends to interact with the Firm, thereby forming <term>Zhen</term><note>The Arousing (Thunder)</note> and <term>Xun</term><note>The Gentle (Wind)</note> <gloss>Thunder and Wind provoke each other</gloss>, and <term>Kan</term><note>The Abysmal (Water)</note> and <term>Li</term><note>The Clinging (Fire)</note> <gloss>Water and Fire do not combat each other</gloss>.

Heaven holds the Yang and hangs the Sun and the Stars <gloss>Li represents the Sun; Dui represents the Stars</gloss>. Those observing the heavens look upward and reflect; therefore, the four celestial images—Qian, Dui, Li, and Zhen—are generated from the top down. 

Earth holds the Yin and carves out the mountains and rivers <gloss>Gen represents the Mountains; Kan represents the Rivers</gloss>. Those on the Earth look downward and observe the natural order; therefore, the four terrestrial images—Kun, Gen, Kan, and Xun—are generated from the bottom up. 

When the Eight Trigrams intermingle—those above crossing to the left and those below crossing to the right—then Qian is in the South, Kun is in the North, Li is in the East, and Kan is in the West. Thus, the image of the <term>Circular Diagram of the Pre-heaven Eight Trigrams</term><note>original: "先天八卦圓圖" (Xiantian Bagua Yuantu). This arrangement represents the ideal, primordial state of the universe in perfect balance, attributed to Fuxi.</note> is established. 

By observing Zhen and Gen in relation to one another, we see that reversing Zhen forms Gen, and reversing Gen forms Zhen. In this arrangement, Qian, Kun, Gen, and Xun occupy the corners, while Kan, Li, Zhen, and Dui occupy the [cardinal directions]...

<summary>This page explains the generative process of the Eight Trigrams through the interaction of Yin and Yang forces and describes their spatial arrangement in the "Pre-heaven" circular diagram. It links the trigrams to natural phenomena like the sun, stars, mountains, and rivers based on whether they are "celestial" or "terrestrial" in origin.</summary>
<keywords>Four Images, Eight Trigrams, Pre-heaven arrangement, Qian and Kun, Zhen and Gen, cosmic generation, celestial vs. terrestrial</keywords>

# Imperial Selection of the Complete Library in the Four Branches of Literature
<meta>Middle Volume</meta>

...and thus the imagery of the <term>Later Heaven</term><note>original: "後天" (Houtian). The "Later Heaven" or "Post-natal" arrangement describes the world of physical manifestation and seasonal change, attributed to King Wen.</note> Eight Trigrams' Square Diagram is revealed. From this, movement and stillness provide mutual support; the Pre-heaven and Later Heaven sequences form each other’s essence; and the circular and square diagrams transform into one another. Through these, the profound meaning of the creation of Heaven and Earth is so vast that it can never be fully exhausted.

Master Shao <note>Shao Yong (1011–1077 AD), a philosopher famous for his "Number and Sequence" approach to the I Ching, which mapped the cosmos through mathematical patterns.</note> said: <term>Qian</term> [Heaven] and <term>Kun</term> [Earth] are the Great Parents <gloss>refer to the Diagram of the Four Images</gloss>, while <term>Fu</term> [Return] and <term>Gou</term> [Coming to Meet] are the Small Parents <gloss>refer to the Diagram of the Eight Trigrams</gloss>. The <term>I Ching</term><note>The Book of Changes</note> is rooted in the primal forces of Heaven and Earth, but it is born into action through the states of "Return" and "Encounter." This is because when the Firm [Yang] interacts with the Yielding [Yin], it creates the hexagram of <term>Fu</term><note>Return (Hexagram 24), symbolizing the first spark of Yang returning to a Yin environment.</note>; when the Yielding interacts with the Firm, it creates the hexagram of <term>Gou</term><note>Coming to Meet (Hexagram 44), symbolizing the first line of Yin entering a Yang environment.</note>. From these initial intersections, the cycles of change proceed infinitely.

He also stated: Among the symbols of the Eight Trigrams, four are <term>immutable</term><note>original: "不易" (buyi). These symbols—Heaven, Earth, Water, and Fire—remain identical even if the trigram is turned upside down.</note> <gloss>Qian, Kun, Kan, and Li</gloss>. Two are <term>reversible</term><note>original: "反易" (fanyi). These shapes change into another trigram when inverted.</note> <gloss>Zhen becomes Gen when inverted; Dui becomes Xun when inverted</gloss>. In this way, six fundamental forms undergo transformations to become the full Eight Trigrams.

<summary>This page explains the transition to the Later Heaven Square Diagram and cites Master Shao Yong to define the "Great" and "Small" parental relationships of the trigrams. It also notes a mathematical curiosity: that the eight trigrams are actually derived from only six distinct shapes, as four are symmetrical and four are inverted pairs.</summary>
<keywords>Later Heaven, Square Diagram, Shao Yong, Qian and Kun, Fu and Gou, Symmetry, Trigram Inversion, I Ching cosmology</keywords>

### Trigram Drawings: Part Four

## Diagram of the Pre-heaven Eight Trigrams in Opposition
<note>original: "先天八卦對待之圖" (Xiantian Bagua Duidai Zhi Tu). The "Pre-heaven" or "Earlier Heaven" arrangement is traditionally attributed to the mythical sage Fuxi. It represents the cosmic order in a state of perfect balance and symmetry.</note>

->**The Eight Trigrams**<-

| Left Side (Yang Sequence) | Right Side (Yin Sequence) |
| :--- | :--- |
| **1. Qian** [Heaven] | **5. Xun** [Wind] |
| **2. Dui** [Lake] | **6. Kan** [Water] |
| **3. Li** [Fire] | **7. Gen** [Mountain] |
| **4. Zhen** [Thunder] | **8. Kun** [Earth] |

<note>The numbers 1 through 8 follow the "Shao Yong" or binary sequence, which shows how the trigrams are mathematically generated from a single line. In this circular "Opposition" diagram, trigrams are placed across from their polar opposites (e.g., Heaven/1 vs. Earth/8) to show a universe in equilibrium.</note>

->**The Core Principle**<-

**The Yang Form** <term>Yang Yi</term><note>The primary positive or active principle in the universe.</note>
**The Firm Quality** <term>Gang</term><note>The characteristic of strength and solidity associated with Yang forces.</note>

**The Yin Form** <term>Yin Yi</term><note>The primary negative or receptive principle in the universe.</note>
**The Soft Quality** <term>柔 Rou</term><note>The characteristic of flexibility and yielding associated with Yin forces.</note>

<note>This central frame explains that all eight trigrams are born from these two "Forms" (Yi). The diagram shows four trigrams connected to the "Yang Form" and four to the "Yin Form," illustrating that even in the complexity of the world, everything retains its fundamental nature of being either "Firm" or "Soft."</note>

<summary>This page illustrates the Pre-heaven Eight Trigrams (Xiantian Bagua) arranged in a balanced, oppositional circle. It highlights the philosophical derivation of these eight symbols from the two primary forces of Yang (Firmness) and Yin (Softness).</summary>

<keywords>Pre-heaven Trigrams, Fuxi Arrangement, Yang Yi, Yin Yi, Firmness and Softness, Shao Yong, I Ching Cosmology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical and philosophical explanation of the Pre-heaven Eight Trigrams, moving from the visual arrangement discussed on the previous page to its underlying generative logic and its metaphysical significance as described by the Song Dynasty scholar Shao Yong.</meta>

### Middle Volume

The diagram to the right originates from the previous section where the **Four Symbols** generate the **Eight Trigrams**. Those elements positioned above the **Yang Instrument** <term>Yang Yi</term><note>The solid, undivided line representing active cosmic energy.</note> intersect on the left, while those below the **Yin Instrument** <term>Yin Yi</term><note>The broken line representing receptive cosmic energy.</note> intersect on the right to form this specific arrangement. 

Master Kangjie <note>The posthumous name of Shao Yong (1011–1077), a philosopher who pioneered the structural and mathematical study of the I Ching.</note> said: 

->**The Pre-heaven Directions**<-

*   **Kun** [Earth] is in the North
*   **Qian** [Heaven] is in the South
*   **Li** [Fire] is in the East
*   **Kan** [Water] is in the West
*   **Zhen** [Thunder] is in the Northeast
*   **Dui** [Lake] is in the Southeast
*   **Xun** [Wind] is in the Southwest
*   **Gen** [Mountain] is in the Northwest

These directions refer specifically to this arrangement. The circle **〇** in the center represents the **Great Ultimate** <term>Taiji</term><note>The primordial source of all existence before the universe divided into Yin and Yang.</note>. The <insert>⚊</insert> and <insert>⚋</insert> represent the **Two Instruments** <note>The primary lines: Yang (solid) and Yin (broken).</note>. 

In the previous diagrams, the symbols used odd and even lines to determine the positions of "above" and "below." By summarizing Heaven, Earth, the Four Symbols, and the Eight Trigrams, they all converge in the center. This is what is meant by the saying: 

> "When contracted, it does not even fill a single grasp; though there are ten thousand differences, they stem from one root." 

In this specific diagram, Yin and Yang are arranged as the "left and right gates." By projecting outward through movement and stillness, the four seasons, and the eight directions, the system reaches the exterior. This is what is meant by: 

> "When expanded, it fills the six directions <term>Liu He</term><note>The universe, defined by the four cardinal points plus up and down.</note>; from one root, ten thousand differences arise."

<summary>This page explains the mathematical generation and spatial orientation of the Eight Trigrams according to Shao Yong, emphasizing the Neo-Confucian principle that the entire complex universe (ten thousand things) originates from a single, unified source (the Great Ultimate).</summary>
<keywords>Shao Yong, Master Kangjie, Taiji, Great Ultimate, Pre-heaven, Eight Trigrams, Yin and Yang, Four Symbols, Ten Thousand Things, One Root</keywords>

### Diagrams and Explanations of the Images of the I Ching: Inner Chapters

*The Announcement of Tang* <note>A chapter in the *Book of History* (Shangshu) recording the speech of King Tang, founder of the Shang Dynasty.</note> says: "The Great Sovereign Lord on High has bestowed a moral center upon the common people; to possess this is to have a **constant nature**." Master Liu <note>Liu Kang-gong, a figure from the Spring and Autumn period.</note> said: "The people receive the 'center' of Heaven and Earth in order to be born." **Confucius** said: "Of all the natures produced by Heaven and Earth, humanity is the most noble." 

The philosopher Zisi <note>The grandson of Confucius, traditionally credited with writing the *Doctrine of the Mean*.</note> said: "What Heaven ordains is called the **nature**." This "center" and this "nature" are what Heaven has granted to me. 

Examining the *Diagram of Generation and Transformation*, one sees that Heaven, Earth, the **Four Symbols**, the **Eight Trigrams**, and the myriad things are all complete within me. This is what Master Cheng <note>Cheng Yi (1033–1107), a foundational philosopher of Neo-Confucianism.</note> called "the innate, self-existent center," and what **Mencius** meant when he said, "The myriad things are all complete within me." It is also what **Zengzi** <note>A disciple of Confucius known for his focus on filial piety and self-cultivation.</note> called "loyalty" and what the Master [Confucius] called "the One"—the underlying principle is nothing other than this.

The **Pre-heaven Diagram** <note>A circular arrangement of the trigrams attributed to the sage Fuxi, representing the ideal, primordial order of the universe.</note> moves from one to two, from two to four, and from four to eight, extending until it reaches hundreds, thousands, tens of thousands, and hundreds of millions—into infinity. 

Early scholars said that the "mind is the **Great Ultimate**" <term>Taiji</term>, possessing all principles and responding to all matters. This is also what Mencius referred to as "striving for empathy" <term>Qiang Shu</term> <note>Specifically, the practice of altruism or reciprocity as a way to realize the interconnectedness of all things.</note>.

<summary>This page connects the mathematical expansion of the I Ching's trigrams to the internal moral nature of humanity, arguing that the structure of the cosmos (the Great Ultimate) is fully contained within the individual mind.</summary>

### Imperial Edition of the Complete Library in Four Sections: Middle Volume

And to practice it: what **Zengzi** called "reciprocity" <note>original: 恕 (*shu*). In Confucian thought, this is the "golden rule" or empathy—treating others as one would wish to be treated.</note> and what **the Master** <note>Confucius.</note> called "pervading" <note>original: 貫 (*guan*). This refers to the "One Thread" (一以貫之) mentioned in the *Analects*, signifying a single underlying principle that connects all of his teachings.</note>—the Way is nothing other than this.

If scholars approach this with an open mind to contemplate it, and turn inward to embody it, they will truly see this principle and truly attain this Way. By silently realizing it, the profound store of **Virtue and Nature and Destiny**, and the very origins of **Rites and Music**, and **Punishment and Governance**, are all found within this.

# Diagram of the Cyclical Flow of the Later Heaven Eight Trigrams 
<note>original: 後天八卦流行之圖 (*Houtian Bagua Liuxing zhi Tu*). The "Later Heaven" arrangement is traditionally attributed to King Wen of Zhou. Unlike the "Earlier Heaven" arrangement which represents a timeless, primordial balance, this "Later Heaven" sequence represents the dynamic movement of the universe, the change of seasons, and the interaction of energy in the physical world.</note>

| **Southeast** | **South** | **Southwest** |
| :--- | :---: | ---: |
| **Xun** <note>The Wind / Wood</note> | **Li** <note>Fire / Light</note> | **Kun** <note>The Earth / Receptive</note> |
| ☴ | ☲ | ☷ |
| | | |
| **East** | | **West** |
| **Zhen** <note>Thunder / The Awakening</note> | | **Dui** <note>The Lake / Joy</note> |
| ☳ | | ☱ |
| | | |
| **Northeast** | **North** | **Northwest** |
| **Gen** <note>The Mountain / Stillness</note> | **Kan** <note>Water / The Abysmal</note> | **Qian** <note>Heaven / The Creative</note> |
| ☶ | ☵ | ☰ |

->(The center is left empty to represent the pivot of change)<-

<note>In this arrangement, the sequence follows the growth of the year: **Zhen** (East/Spring) marks the beginning of growth, **Li** (South/Summer) marks the peak of light, **Dui** (West/Autumn) marks the harvest, and **Kan** (North/Winter) marks the period of storage and rest.</note>

<summary>This page illustrates the Later Heaven (King Wen) arrangement of the eight trigrams, a fundamental map in Chinese metaphysics used to track the movement of energy through space and time. It marks a shift from abstract philosophy to the structural application of the I Ching’s symbols.</summary>

<keywords>Later Heaven, Eight Trigrams, King Wen Sequence, Bagua, Xun, Li, Kun, Zhen, Dui, Gen, Kan, Qian, Cosmology</keywords>

--- Page 66 ---
<meta>This page provides the textual explanation for the "Diagram of the Cyclical Flow of the Later Heaven Eight Trigrams" shown on the previous page. It explains the spatial logic of the trigrams and their alignment with the Five Elements and the numerology of the Yellow River Map (He Tu).</meta>

### Imperial Sanctioned Complete Library of the Four Treasuries, Middle Volume

The preceding "Diagram of the Four Images Generating the Eight Trigrams" <note>This refers to the developmental sequence where the Four Images—Major/Minor Yin and Yang—split further into the eight fundamental symbols of the *I Ching*.</note> shows that if one views the four central trigrams from an inverse perspective, they represent **Zhen** <term>Thunder</term>, **Dui** <term>Lake</term>, **Kan** <term>Water</term>, and **Li** <term>Fire</term>. If the four surrounding trigrams are viewed from a direct perspective, they are **Qian** <term>Heaven</term>, **Kun** <term>Earth</term>, **Gen** <term>Mountain</term>, and **Xun** <term>Wind</term>.

Therefore, in this symbolic configuration, **Kan**, **Li**, **Zhen**, and **Dui** occupy the four cardinal directions <note>North, South, East, and West</note>, while **Qian**, **Kun**, **Gen**, and **Xun** occupy the four corners or ordinal directions <note>Northwest, Southwest, Northeast, and Southeast</note>. 

When we examine this according to the *He Tu* <note>original: 河圖 (*He Tu*). The "Yellow River Map" is a prehistoric numerological diagram. It uses dots to represent numbers and is considered the mathematical basis for the *I Ching* and the Five Elements.</note>, the numbers **one and six** are situated in the North as the element Water, which the trigram **Kan** corresponds to; for **Kan** is Water. The numbers **two and seven** are situated in the South as the element Fire, which the trigram **Li** corresponds to; for **Li** is Fire. 

The numbers **three and eight** are situated in the East as the element Wood, which the trigram **Zhen** corresponds to; **Zhen** is Thunder, the vital energy that stirs and moves in the Spring. The numbers **four and nine** are situated in the West as the element Metal, which the trigram **Dui** corresponds to; **Dui** is the Lake, associated with the gathering and contracting energy of Autumn. 

**Qian** represents cold and ice; it is located in the Northwest, adjacent to **Dui**, and thus shares the nature of the element Metal. **Xun** represents the lifting wind; it is located in the Southeast, adjacent to **Zhen**, and thus shares the nature of the element Wood. The numbers **five and ten** reside in the center as the element Earth, from which **Kun** <gloss>the Earth</gloss> and **Gen** <gloss>the Mountain</gloss> are divided...

<summary>This text explains the spatial and elemental logic of the Later Heaven trigram arrangement, mapping the trigrams to cardinal directions, seasons, and the numerology of the Yellow River Map. It highlights how the trigrams represent the cyclical movement of natural forces like spring thunder and autumn harvest.</summary>
<keywords>Later Heaven Eight Trigrams, He Tu, Yellow River Map, Five Elements, Zhen, Li, Dui, Kan, Qian, Xun, Kun, Gen, Siku Quanshu</keywords>

### Illustrated Explanation of the I Ching Images: Inner Chapter

...responds to; for **Zhen** <term>Thunder</term> is Wood. **Kun** <term>Earth</term> belongs to the Yin <note>The receptive, dark, or feminine principle</note> category; therefore, by examining its classification, it is relegated to the Southwest. **Gen** <term>Mountain</term> belongs to the Yang <note>The active, light, or masculine principle</note> category; likewise, by examining its classification, it is established in the Northeast. This is the reasoning by which the positions of the **Later Heaven Eight Trigrams** were determined. Therefore, by coordinating them with the seasons of Heaven, verifying them with the advantages of the Earth, and examining them through human affairs, the sequence of the rotation of the four seasonal energies can be understood.

The *Shuogua* <note>original: 說卦 (*Shuogua*); one of the "Ten Wings" or ancient commentaries of the I Ching that explains the attributes and symbols of each trigram.</note> says: "**Gen** is that through which all things achieve their end and their beginning." Things cannot exist without a ruler; therefore, "The Lord comes forth in **Zhen**." **Zhen** represents the East, the place where the sun rises. 

Regarding the one who presides over the ritual vessels <note>A metaphor for the heir who maintains the ancestral sacrifices and takes leadership of the household or state</note>, none is more suited than the Eldest Son <note>The trigram Zhen is traditionally associated with the eldest son</note>. When the Eldest Son manages affairs, he is paired with the Eldest Daughter <note>The trigram Xun</note>; therefore, the sequence follows with **Xun** <term>Wind</term>. This presents the image of the Lineage Heir and his Consort. 

Once the family is regulated, the state can be governed. Proceeding from the family to the state, the sequence continues with **Li** <term>Fire</term>. **Li** signifies clarity and brightness. This is the image of the **Sage facing South** to listen to the world <note>original: 南面 (*nanmian*); the traditional ritual position of a Chinese Emperor, who faces South to receive his subjects</note> and the image of great brilliance in the center of the sky.

<summary>This page explains the spatial logic of the Later Heaven trigrams, linking them to the cardinal directions, the seasons, and the Confucian social order of the family and the state.</summary>
<keywords>Later Heaven Trigrams, Shuogua commentary, Zhen (Thunder), Li (Fire), Gen (Mountain), Sage Ruler, Eldest Son, spatial orientation</keywords>

# Imperial Edition of the Complete Library in the Four Branches of Literature: Middle Volume

When the sun reaches its zenith, it begins to decline; for this reason, it is followed by **Kun** <term>The Receptive Earth</term>. **Kun** signifies compliance and submission. To "render service in **Kun**" carries the meaning of resting from one’s work. Therefore, it is followed by **Dui** <term>The Joyous Lake</term>. **Dui** represents the West, the place where the sun sets. As the day turns toward darkness, one enters for rest and relaxation; thus, it is said that "The Lord brings joy in **Dui**" <note>original: 說 (*shuo*), used here in its ancient sense of 悅 (*yue*), meaning joy or pleasure</note>. 

Next follows **乾 Qian** <term>The Creative Heaven</term>. **Qian** is the ultimate Yang <note>The active, light-filled principle</note>, yet the Northwest is a Yin direction. When the forces of Yin and Yang clash against one another, it is said: "The Lord battles in **Qian**." This is followed by **坎 Kan** <term>The Abysmal Water</term>. **Kan** is Water, the trigram of the true North. It represents the hour of midnight and is a symbol of deep, hidden Yin; therefore, it is said that "The Lord is comforted after labor in **Kan**" <note>original: 勞 (*lao*). In this context, it refers to the resting phase of the cycle where work is finished and the spirit is restored</note>. 

Only after labor can there be achievement; thus, it is said: "The Lord brings all things to completion in **Gen**." Consequently, the sequence concludes with **艮 Gen** <term>The Still Mountain</term>.

<summary>This page explains the cyclical progression of the "Later Heaven" trigram arrangement, mapping the trigrams to the movement of the sun and the changing hours of the day. It justifies why the sequence ends with Gen (Mountain) as the point of completion and new beginnings.</summary>

<keywords>Later Heaven Bagua, King Wen Sequence, Kun, Dui, Qian, Kan, Gen, Shuogua, I Ching, Yin and Yang, cosmology, Siku Quanshu</keywords>

<meta>This page continues from the previous diagrammatic representation of the Eight Trigrams, moving from the visual layout to a philosophical justification of the "Later Heaven" or "Human" arrangement. ### Middle Volume

The text to the right is based on the previous "Diagram of the Four Images Generating the Eight Trigrams," yet the positions of the four trigrams on the left and right have been exchanged. **Qian**, **Li**, **Kan**, and **Kun** are situated in the center, representing the imagery of the <term>head, eyes, heart, and abdomen</term>. Meanwhile, **Zhen**, **Gen**, **Xun**, and **Dui** are placed to the left and right, representing the imagery of the <term>hands, feet, thighs, and arms</term>.

Master Xiyi <note>Master Xiyi is the honorary title of Chen Tuan (c. 871–989 CE), a famous Taoist hermit and cosmologist who is said to have rediscovered these ancient diagrams.</note> believed that these forms and categories harmonize because humanity is the <term>combined Qi</term> <note>Qi is the vital life force or energy that constitutes all things in the universe.</note> of Heaven and Earth. As the classics say, "Only the Sovereign [Heaven] bestows the inward heart upon the people, who then possess a constant nature." Therefore, all who discuss the <term>Study of the Dao</term> <note>Daoxue, a term often used for the philosophical pursuit of universal truth and moral cultivation.</note> trace their origins back to this principle.

The *Miscellaneous Notes on the Trigrams* <note>The *Za Gua*, one of the traditional "Ten Wings" or commentaries appended to the *I Ching*.</note> states:
> **Qian** is strong and **Kun** is soft; **Li** moves upward and **Kan** moves downward; **Dui** is manifest and **Xun** is hidden; **Zhen** arises and **Gen** halts.

In investigating this matter, the *Kinship of the Three* <note>The *Cantong Qi*, an ancient text that applies the *I Ching* to the practice of internal alchemy.</note> says:
> **Qian** and **Kun** are the door and gateway of the Changes, and the parents of all the trigrams. **Kan** and **Li** form the outer rim, moving the hub and centering the axle. 

These four "male and female" trigrams <note>Referencing the two pairs of primary opposites: Qian/Kun and Kan/Li.</note> serve as the <term>bellows and pipes</term> <note>original: "橐籥" (tuó yuè). This is a metaphor for the way the universe "breathes" or generates life through the empty but productive movement of the Dao.</note> of existence. This understanding is also derived from the principles shown here. Therefore, I have now illustrated and transmitted this work.

<summary>This page explains the anatomical correspondence of the Eight Trigrams, mapping them onto the human body (e.g., Qian as the head, Kun as the abdomen). It justifies this "human-centered" arrangement by quoting the "Za Gua" and the alchemical "Cantong Qi," emphasizing that humans are the concentrated essence of Heaven and Earth.</summary>
<keywords>Eight Trigrams, Chen Tuan (Master Xiyi), Cantong Qi, Za Gua, Human Anatomy, Qi, Qian and Kun, Kan and Li, Bellows and Pipes</keywords>

* Holding Together <gloss>Water</gloss>
* Splitting Apart <gloss>Mountain</gloss>
* **The Receptive** <gloss>Earth</gloss> <note>Original: 坤 (Kūn).</note>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the "Pre-Heaven" (Xiantian) arrangement of the hexagrams. While the previous page listed specific hexagram groupings by their inner trigrams, this page provides the philosophical and cosmological rationale for the circular arrangement. It draws directly from the "Shuogua Zhuan" (Explanation of the Trigrams), one of the "Ten Wings" of the I Ching, to explain the spatial and energetic relationship between the fundamental forces of the universe.</meta>

## To the Right: The Diagram of the Sixty-Four Hexagrams in Cycles

The *Explanation of the Trigrams* <note>Original: 說卦 (Shuòguà), one of the classic commentaries on the I Ching.</note> states: 

"Heaven and Earth determine the positions; Mountain and Lake communicate their vital breath <term>qi: the circulating life force or energy of the universe</term>; Thunder and Wind approach one another; Water and Fire do not conflict. The eight trigrams intermingle. Counting what has passed is following the natural flow; knowing what is to come is a reverse process. Therefore, the *Book of Changes* is a system of reverse counting."

If one seeks to understand the imagery of the "Pre-Heaven" Circular Diagram of the Sixty-Four Hexagrams: 

**Heaven and Earth determine the positions**, with *Qian* <note>The Creative / Heaven</note> in the South and *Kun* <note>The Receptive / Earth</note> in the North. <note>In traditional Chinese cartography, South is positioned at the top of the map and North at the bottom.</note> **Heaven is honored above, while Earth is humble below.**

**Mountain and Lake communicate their vital breath**: the Mountain <note>Gèn</note> stands firm in the Northwest, while the Lake <note>Duì</note> flows into the Southeast. 

**Thunder and Wind approach one another**: Thunder <note>Zhèn</note> emerges in the Northeast, and Wind <note>Xùn</note> flourishes in the Southwest. 

**Water and Fire do not conflict**: *Li* <note>Fire</note> descends toward the East <gloss>The Sun, the Great Brightness</gloss>, and *Kan* <note>Water</note> rises toward the West <gloss>The Moon, the Lesser Brightness</gloss>. 

This describes the internal imagery, which focuses on stillness and the stabilization of positions. **The eight trigrams intermingle**: "Intermingling" refers to the way they intersect and interact with one another, arranged with one on the left and one on the right.

<summary>This page explains the "Pre-Heaven" circular arrangement of the hexagrams, using the "Shuogua" commentary to justify the spatial placement of the eight primary trigrams as a reflection of cosmic balance. It establishes a map of the universe where opposing forces—like Fire and Water or Heaven and Earth—exist in a state of harmonious interaction rather than conflict.</summary>

<keywords>Shuogua, Pre-Heaven arrangement, Xiantian, circular diagram, trigrams, Qian and Kun, cosmic balance, reverse counting, I Ching cosmology</keywords>

--- Page 73 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the spatial orientation of the eight trigrams (Qian, Kun, etc.) to their dynamic interaction. The previous page established where the fundamental forces "sit" in the Pre-Heaven arrangement; this page explains how they "exchange" or "intermingle" to generate the full cycle of the sixty-four hexagrams, comparing their movement to the rotation of the stars and the passage of time.</meta>

### Volume Two

*Qian* <note>Heaven</note> exchanges with *Xun* <note>Wind</note> and *Xun* exchanges with *Qian*; *Kan* <note>Water</note> exchanges with *Dui* <note>Lake</note> and *Dui* exchanges with *Kan*; *Li* <note>Fire</note> exchanges with *Gen* <note>Mountain</note> and *Gen* exchanges with *Li*; *Kun* <note>Earth</note> exchanges with *Zhen* <note>Thunder</note> and *Zhen* exchanges with *Kun*.

<gloss>The trigrams belonging to the **Yang Instrument** <term>Yang Yi: the primary division representing light, activity, and maleness</term>—Qian, Dui, Li, and Zhen—originally belong on the left side, but now they mutually occupy the right side. The trigrams of the **Yin Instrument** <term>Yin Yi: the primary division representing darkness, receptivity, and femaleness</term> originally belong on the right side, but now they mutually occupy the left side. Because of this...</gloss>

<gloss>...Xun, Kan, Gen, and Kun, the trigrams of the Yin Instrument, which originally belong on the right, now mutually occupy the left; likewise, the Yang trigrams originally on the left now mutually occupy the right. Thus, "hardness and softness rub against each other, and the eight trigrams activate one another," and transformations are endless. The detailed explanation is found below.</gloss>

This speaks from the perspective of "outer imagery," which focuses on movement and the progression of time. Through circular rotation and mutual pushing, the sixty-four hexagrams are formed, surrounding the eight directions, much like the **Twenty-Eight Lunar Mansions** <note>Traditional Chinese constellations used for mapping the sky</note> are distributed across the **Twelve Earthly Branches** <note>The twelve divisions of the Chinese zodiac used to track hours, months, and years</note>.

Therefore, if one counts by moving to the right, the eight trigrams—*Qian, Dui, Li, Zhen, Kun, Gen, Kan, and Xun*—move from the inside to the outside like a ring without an end. "Counting what has passed is following the natural flow" <note>A reference to the *Shuogua Zhuan* regarding the movement of time</note> refers to hexagrams that have already passed. "Following the flow" means adhering to the sequence of the eight trigrams, just as one tracks previous days starting from today, or tracks time from the **Winter Solstice**...

<summary>This page explains the "interchanging" logic of the trigrams in the circular Pre-Heaven diagram, describing how Yin and Yang forces swap positions to create perpetual motion. It compares this metaphysical rotation to the physical movement of constellations and the calendar.</summary>

<keywords>Eight Trigrams, Yang Yi, Yin Yi, circular arrangement, Pre-Heaven Diagram, Twenty-Eight Lunar Mansions, Twelve Earthly Branches, cosmic rotation, I Ching cosmology</keywords>

### Diagrammatic Explanation of the Study of Changes: Inner Chapters

...and when counting through the cycles of the **Start of Winter** and the **Autumn Equinox**, one moves from *Kun* <note>Earth</note> to *Gen* <note>Mountain</note>, to *Kan* <note>Water</note>, to *Xun* <note>Wind</note>, and then to *Qian* <note>Heaven</note>. These all follow their natural order as they are counted. To "**know what is coming is a backward calculation**" <note>original: 知來者逆</note> refers to the future approaching from ahead. To calculate "backward" or "in reverse" means to go against the standard order of the eight trigrams. This is like being in the present day and calculating in reverse to understand the days to come. From the **Winter Solstice**, counting toward the **Start of Spring** and the **Spring Equinox**, one moves from *Kun* <note>Earth</note> to *Zhen* <note>Thunder</note>, to *Li* <note>Fire</note>, to *Dui* <note>Lake</note>, and then to *Qian* <note>Heaven</note>. All of these are understood by counting against the sequence. 

In general, all calculations within the *Changes* are derived from this reverse extrapolation: the individual lines <note>the six stacked lines that make up a hexagram</note> are generated from the bottom to the top, and the hexagrams are counted from right to left. Therefore, it is said: "**The *I Ching* is a system of reverse counting.**"

Note: This diagram uses the **Pre-Heaven Diagram** <term>Xian Tian Tu: the primordial arrangement of the trigrams representing the universe in an ideal, balanced state before manifestation</term> in a layout where one is upright and one is inverted. The eight trigrams of the lower body remain still and keep their assigned positions, while the eight trigrams of the upper body are active and rotate to the right. Through this process, "**hardness and softness rub against each other, and the eight trigrams agitate one another**" <note>This is a famous quote from the *Great Commentary* of the *I Ching* describing how the interaction of opposing forces creates all phenomena</note>. While one trigram remains the stable foundation...

<summary>This page explains the metaphysical principle of "reverse counting" (ni shu), which allows a practitioner to calculate future events by moving against the standard sequence of trigrams. It also describes the mechanical interaction of the Pre-Heaven Diagram, where a stationary lower set of trigrams interacts with a rotating upper set to create change.</summary>

<keywords>Reverse Counting, Pre-Heaven Diagram, Trigram Rotation, Winter Solstice, Hardness and Softness, I Ching mathematical theory</keywords>

# Imperially Commissioned Complete Library of the Four Treasuries: Middle Volume

**Water** <note>Kan</note>, **Fire** <note>Li</note>, **After Fording** <note>Ji Ji</note>, **Before Fording** <note>Wei Ji</note>, **Following** <note>Sui</note>, **Work on what has been Spoiled** <note>Gu</note>, **Development** <note>Jian</note>, **The Marrying Maiden** <note>Gui Mei</note>, **Nourishment** <note>Yi</note>, **Preponderance of the Great** <note>Da Guo</note>, **Inner Truth** <note>Zhong Fu</note>, and **Preponderance of the Small** <note>Xiao Guo</note>—altogether sixteen hexagrams.
<column-break/>
The lines of these hexagrams transform through inversion and opposition <note>this refers to the mathematical relationship where flipping a hexagram upside down or changing its polarities creates its partner</note>; they alternate across three positions. They are linked together like a string of pearls or like a net hanging from its headrope—orderly and without confusion.
<column-break/>
Observing the wonder of this natural design, one realizes it is not something that human effort could create. Therefore, if we discuss this in terms of the seasonal nodes of a single year: **Thunder** <note>Zhen</note> first interacts with **Earth** <note>Kun</note>. A single Yang line <term>Yang: the active, bright, or masculine principle, represented by a solid line</term> is born at the hexagram of the **Winter Solstice**, where **Earth** transforms into **Return** <note>Fu</note>.
<column-break/>
Because of this, the influence of **Heaven** <note>Qian</note> begins at the "Initial Nine" <note>the first line</note> of **Return** and reaches its completion at the midpoint of Noon <note>the height of Summer</note>. **Wind** <note>Xun</note> begins to dissipate the force of Heaven. A single Yin line <term>Yin: the receptive, dark, or feminine principle, represented by a broken line</term> is born at the hexagram of the **Summer Solstice**, where **Heaven** transforms into **Coming to Meet** <note>Gou</note>.
<column-break/>
Because of this, the influence of **Earth** <note>Kun</note> begins at the "Initial Six" <note>the first line</note> of **Coming to Meet** and reaches its completion at the midpoint of Midnight <note>the depth of Winter</note>. **Heaven and Earth** establish the fixed positions of Above and Below. At the Winter Solstice, the transformation of Earth involves an abundance of Yin; it is very cold, the day is at its shortest, and the night is at its longest. At the Summer Solstice, the transformation of Heaven involves an abundance of Yang; it is very hot, the day is at its longest, and the night is at its shortest.

# Inner Volume of the Illustrated Explanation of the Changes' Images

->Sixteen<-

The two solstices of Winter and Summer are where **Yin** and **Yang** <term>Yin and Yang: the fundamental dual forces of Chinese philosophy—dark/receptive and light/active</term> merge. **Heaven** <note>Qian</note> and **Earth** <note>Kun</note> intersect in the center to become the hexagrams of **Water** <note>Kan</note> and **Fire** <note>Li</note>. **Water** and **Fire** then intersect to become **After Fording** <note>Ji Ji</note> and **Before Fording** <note>Wei Ji</note>. These two—**After Fording** and **Before Fording**—intersect in the middle space between North and South. At the **Spring Equinox**, **After Fording** transforms into **Limitation** <note>Jie</note>. 

Because of this, the influence of **Water** <note>Kan</note> begins at the "Nine in the Second Place" <note>the second line of the hexagram, which is a solid Yang line</note> of **Limitation** and reaches its completion at the midpoint of the Hour of the Rooster <note>the West, or roughly 5–7 PM</note>. At the **Autumn Equinox**, **Before Fording** transforms into **Traveling** <note>Lü</note>. 

Because of this, the influence of **Fire** <note>Li</note> begins at the "Six in the Second Place" <note>the second line of the hexagram, which is a broken Yin line</note> of **Traveling** and reaches its completion at the midpoint of the Hour of the Rabbit <note>the East, or 5–7 AM</note>. These periods contain three **Yang** lines and three **Yin** lines; the warmth and coolness are perfectly balanced, and day and night are of equal length. At the two equinoxes of Spring and Autumn, **Yin** and **Yang** begin to separate. 

At the **Beginning of Spring** <term>Li Chun: the first of the 24 solar terms, marking the start of the traditional Chinese spring</term>, **Nourishment** <note>Yi</note> transforms into **Grace** <note>Bi</note>. In the hexagram of **Nourishment**, two **Yang** lines are on the outside while four **Yin** lines are on the inside. At the **Beginning of Summer**, **Inner Truth** <note>Zhong Fu</note> transforms into **Small Taming** <note>Xiao Chu</note>. In **Inner Truth**, four **Yang** lines are on the outside while two **Yin** lines are on the inside. 

This represents the period of Spring and Summer, where **Yang** is on the outside and **Yin** is on the inside. This is the time when the Sages nourish the **Yang** energy. At the **Beginning of Autumn**, **Preponderance of the Great** <note>Da Guo</note> transforms into **Oppression** <note>Kun</note>. In **Preponderance of the Great**, two **Yin** lines <insert>are on the outside...</insert>
