# Imperially Commissioned Complete Library of the Four Treasuries
## Middle Volume

...on the outside, while the four **Yang** <term>Yang: the light, active, masculine principle</term> lines stand within. At the **Beginning of Winter** <term>Li Dong: the solar term marking the start of winter, usually around November 7th</term>, **Preponderance of the Small** <note>Xiao Guo, Hexagram 62</note> transforms into **Enthusiasm** <note>Yu, Hexagram 16</note>. In **Preponderance of the Small**, four **Yin** <term>Yin: the dark, receptive, feminine principle</term> lines are on the outside while two **Yang** lines are on the inside. This illustrates how, during autumn and winter, **Yang** energy is contained within while **Yin** energy is on the exterior. This corresponds to the time when the Sages "nurture **Yin** during autumn and winter." Thus, the stages of the year’s full celestial orbit are all accounted for in the diagram; this is what Master Shao <note>Shao Yong, a famous Song dynasty philosopher known for his work on the mathematical structure of the universe</note> meant when he said that the four seasons and the changing lengths of day and night all originate from this logic.

Furthermore, if we calculate based on the cycle of a single month, the time of the **Doubled Earth** <note>Kun, Hexagram 2, representing pure Yin</note> corresponds to the interval between the dark moon and the new moon. From this point, light is born in sequence. In the **Thunder** trigram aspect of **Preponderance of the Small** <gloss>The two are the inverse of Thunder</gloss>, on the third day of the lunar month at dusk, the moon appears in the west at the position of *Geng* <note>a directional marker in the Ten Heavenly Stems system</note>. In the **Lake** trigram aspect of **Preponderance of the Great** <note>Da Guo, Hexagram 28</note> <gloss>The two are the inverse of Lake</gloss>, on the eighth day, the moon reaches its **First Quarter** <term>Shang Xian: the half-moon during the waxing phase</term>, appearing in the south at the position of *Ding*. By the fifteenth day, the form of **Heaven** <note>Qian, Hexagram 1, representing pure Yang</note> achieves the **Full Moon** <term>Wang: the point of maximum illumination</term>; it is perfectly full and rises in the east from the region of *Jia*. From this point, the **Waning Shadow** <term>Po: literally the 'soul' or 'dark shadow' of the moon, referring to the beginning of the waning phase</term> gradually begins to grow. In the **Wind** trigram aspect of **Inner Truth** <note>Zhong Fu, Hexagram 61</note> <gloss>The two are the inverse of Wind</gloss>, on the eighteenth day at dawn, the moon is seen in the west at the position of *Xin*.

# Explanations of the Images in the Book of Changes: Inner Chapters

In the **Nourishing** hexagram <note>Yi, Hexagram 27</note> which relates to the **Mountain** <term>Gen: the trigram representing stillness and the mountain</term> <gloss>the inverse of two Mountain trigrams</gloss>, on the twenty-third day of the lunar month, the Last Quarter moon aligns directly south of the *Bing* position <note>original: "丙" (Bing), a directional marker in the Ten Heavenly Stems system associated with the south</note>. In this way, the movements and degrees of the **Moon** <term>Tai Yin: literally "Great Yin," the celestial body representing the receptive, feminine principle</term> for an entire month are fully accounted for in the diagram. This is what Master Shao <note>Shao Yong (1011–1077), a philosopher famous for his mathematical approach to the universe</note> meant when he said that the waxing and waning of the movements of the dark moon, new moon, and the quarters all originate from this logic.

As for the **Mountain** trigram, it is the hexagram of the Northeast. It is the place where the myriad things "bring the end to completion and bring the beginning to completion." Therefore, the hexagrams associated with the **Winter Solstice** conclude with the **Mountain** aspect <gloss>Mountain over Thunder; the Hexagram of **Nourishing**</gloss>, and the seasonal nodes of the **Beginning of Spring** <term>Li Chun: the first solar term of the year, occurring around February 4th</term> commence from the **Mountain** aspect <gloss>Mountain over Fire; the Hexagram of **Adornment**</gloss>. This is the natural order of numbers. Thus it is said: "The *Book of Changes* is a standard for the patterns of Heaven and Earth."

Master Shao said: "Although this diagram has no text, I could speak of it all day long without ever departing from its logic." This is because the principles of Heaven, Earth, and all things are entirely contained within it.

Master Zhu <note>Zhu Xi (1130–1200), the most influential Neo-Confucian scholar</note> said: "Regarding the **Diagram of Preceding Heaven** <note>original: "先天圖" (Xiantian Tu), a circular arrangement of hexagrams representing the primordial, natural order of the universe</note>, if we speak of it in terms of the cycle of a single year—or more broadly, from the ancient past to the present, over ten...

It emphasizes that the mathematical "Diagram of Preceding Heaven" contains all universal principles according to masters Shao Yong and Zhu Xi.</summary>

# Imperial Edition of the Complete Library of the Four Treasuries

### Volume Two

Twenty-nine thousand six hundred years <note>This refers to a vast cosmic cycle of time. While the standard Great Year of philosopher Shao Yong is 129,600 years, the text here emphasizes that regardless of the scale, the cyclical nature remains the same.</note> is merely this same circle. Even a single day of twelve hours <term>Shi: a traditional unit of time equal to two modern hours</term> is likewise just this circle. All cycles are calculated by projecting upward from the hexagram **Return** <term>Fu: Hexagram 24, representing the Winter Solstice and the first stirring of light</term>.

->The Commentary says:
**Heaven** is noble and **Earth** is humble; thus the positions of **The Creative** <term>Qian: the trigram representing pure Yang, strength, and the heavens</term> and **The Receptive** <term>Kun: the trigram representing pure Yin, devotion, and the earth</term> are settled. As the low and the high are displayed, the ranks of the noble and the humble are established. Movement and stillness have their constancy; thus the **Firm** and the **Yielding** are clearly distinguished. Matters are gathered by their kind and things are divided into their groups; thus good and bad fortune arise. In the heavens, celestial images are formed; on the earth, physical shapes are formed; in this way, the process of change and transformation is made visible. Therefore, the **Firm** and the **Yielding** rub against one another, and the **Eight Trigrams** <term>Ba Gua: the eight foundational symbols of the I Ching representing natural forces</term> activate each other. They are drummed by thunder and lightning, and moistened by wind and rain. The Sun and Moon run their course; first comes the cold, then comes the heat. The Way of **The Creative** forms the male; the Way of **The Receptive** forms the female.<-

The passage above is the first chapter of the **Appended Phrases** <term>Xi Ci: a seminal philosophical commentary that explains the metaphysics behind the hexagrams</term>. It serves to explain the profound meaning of the **Circular Diagram of the Sixty-Four Hexagrams of the Earlier Heaven** <note>The "Earlier Heaven" (Xian Tian) arrangement is a primordial map of the universe where opposites are balanced, attributed to the legendary sage Fuxi</note>.

# Eighteenth Page

<margin>
*Inner Chapters of the Illustrated Explanation of the Changes' Images*
</margin>

**Heaven is noble and Earth is humble; thus the positions of the Creative and the Receptive are settled.** Heaven is clear and resides above, while Earth is turbid and resides below. As the upper is honored and the lower is humble, the positions of **The Creative** <term>Qian: the hexagram/trigram representing Heaven and pure Yang</term> and **The Receptive** <term>Kun: the hexagram/trigram representing Earth and pure Yin</term> are thereby established. **As the low and the high are displayed, the ranks of the noble and the humble are established.** Yang is honored and Yin is humble; the ruler is honored and the minister is humble. As each is displayed in its proper place, the levels of nobility and meanness are thereby divided. **Movement and stillness have their constancy; thus the Firm and the Yielding are clearly distinguished.** The external involves movement while the internal involves stillness; Yang is active while Yin is still. As each adheres to its constant nature, the meaning of the **Firm** and the **Yielding** <term>Gang-Rou: the two primary qualities of the lines in a hexagram, representing hard/active and soft/passive forces</term> is thereby determined. Those that are high are close to Heaven; those that are low are close to Earth. The trigrams **Heaven**, **Lake**, **Fire**, and **Thunder** <note>original: 乾兌離震 (Qian, Dui, Li, Zhen). In the "Pre-Heaven" arrangement, these represent the Yang or ascending half of the cycle.</note> cluster together in the Southeast. The trigrams **Earth**, **Mountain**, **Water**, and **Wind** <note>original: 坤艮坎巽 (Kun, Gen, Kan, Xun). These represent the Yin or descending half.</note> cluster together in the Northwest. Furthermore, the various creatures associated with the **Eight Trigrams** <term>Ba Gua: the eight foundational symbols of Chinese cosmology</term> also follow their respective trigrams to divide into groups—just as horses, oxen, sheep, and pigs each form their own herds. This is what is meant by **"matters cluster together by their kind, and things divide into their groups."**

<margin>
Mr. Zheng of Hesha <note>Zheng Xia (1041–1119), a Song Dynasty scholar known for his diagrams and social commentary.</note> says: "Animals belong to the Yang category, while plants belong to [the Yin category]..."
</margin>

<summary>This page explains the natural and social hierarchies of the world as reflections of the fundamental relationship between Heaven (Yang) and Earth (Yin). It describes how all things—from celestial bodies to livestock—naturally organize themselves into groups based on these cosmic principles.</summary>

<keywords>Qian and Kun, Eight Trigrams, Firm and Yielding, Illustrated Explanation of the Changes' Images, Mr. Zheng, classification of things, cosmology</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical exploration of the I Ching’s cosmological structure, specifically focusing on the categorization of the natural world into groups based on the interaction of Yin and Yang. It transitions from the positioning of Heaven and Earth to the biological and physical classification of all things, culminating in the human position as the most balanced of beings.</meta>

# Imperial Edition of the Complete Library of the Four Treasuries

## Middle Scroll

Those that are mobile yet have Yin qualities cannot be rooted, for they lean toward the **Yang**; those that are rooted cannot be mobile, for they lean toward the **Yin**. <note>The author is categorizing life forms: "mobile" refers to animals, while "rooted" (planted) refers to vegetation. He argues that things leaning toward Yang (active) move, while those leaning toward Yin (passive) stay in one place.</note> The creatures of Yin and Yang are divided into groups based on these principles. Only humans are capable of being both mobile and rooted, and rooted yet mobile; <note>This likely refers to the human ability to stand upright like a plant ("rooted") while possessing the power of movement ("mobile"), representing a perfect balance.</note> therefore, humans obtain the full measure of Yin and Yang and are inherently more spiritual than all other creatures.

**The Creative** <term>Qian: the trigram of pure Heaven/Yang</term> represents the "Yang of Yang," which constitutes the groups of flying birds and walking beasts. **Keeping Still**, **The Abysmal**, and **The Arousing** <note>original: 艮 (Gen), 坎 (Kan), 震 (Zhen)</note> represent the "Yin of Yang," which constitutes the groups of insects and fish.

**The Receptive** <term>Kun: the trigram of pure Earth/Yin</term> represents the "Yin of Yin," which constitutes the groups of earth and stone. **The Gentle**, **The Clinging**, and **The Joyous** <note>original: 巽 (Xun), 離 (Li), 兌 (Dui)</note> represent the "Yang of Yin," which constitutes the groups of herbs and trees.

As Yin, Yang, Firmness, and Yielding—along with Good and Evil—gather and divide, good and bad fortune arise. **Good and bad fortune** are the manifestations of gain and loss. When rain, sunshine, heat, cold, and wind—these five—arrive in completion and each at its proper time, it is a sign of good fortune. If any one of these reaches an extreme of excess, it is a sign of misfortune; likewise, if any one of these is entirely absent, it is also a sign of misfortune. <note>This refers to the "Five Verifications" from ancient Chinese philosophy, where weather patterns were seen as omens of a ruler's virtue or vice.</note> The gains and losses found in human affairs, which manifest as blessings or calamities, also arise from this same principle.

In Heaven, images are formed: these are the sun, moon, stars, and planets. On Earth, shapes are formed: these are water, fire, wood, and metal. Images appear in Heaven and shapes are formed on Earth, and through this, the traces of change and transformation become manifest. Firmness and Yielding rub against one another: One, Seven, Three, and Nine are the numbers of **Firmness**; Two, Six, Four... <note>The text cuts off here, but refers to the Pythagorean-like numerology of the I Ching where odd numbers are Yang/Firm and even numbers are Yin/Yielding.</note>

<summary>This page classifies all existence—animals, plants, minerals, and humans—into a hierarchical system based on the eight trigrams and the balance of Yin and Yang. It emphasizes that human superiority stems from a unique biological balance, and that environmental harmony (weather) is a direct indicator of cosmic and moral fortune.</summary>

<keywords>Yin and Yang, Eight Trigrams, Taxonomy of Nature, Five Elements, Shao Yong, Human Exceptionalism, Omens, Firmness and Yielding</keywords>

--- Page 84 ---
<meta>This page continues the cosmological discussion from the previous section. While the previous page categorized living things (animals, plants, humans) according to their balance of Yin and Yang, this page explains the mechanical interaction of the trigrams—the "rubbing" and "stirring" of cosmic forces—that results in the sixty-four hexagrams and the physical phenomena of our world.</meta>

<margin>
Illustrated Explanation of the Images of the Changes: Inner Book
</margin>

Hard and soft rub against one another. To "rub" <note>original: 摩 (mó)</note> means to grind and rotate; just as objects grind against each other in a rotating motion, these forces interact to form the Eight Trigrams. The Eight Trigrams then "stir" one another. To "stir" <note>original: 盪 (dàng)</note> means to sway or shake; it is like using water to wash a vessel, swirling and shaking it around. Through this swirling interaction, **The Creative, The Receptive, The Abysmal, The Clinging, The Arousing, Keeping Still, The Gentle,** and **The Joyous** <term>The Eight Trigrams: Qian, Kun, Kan, Li, Zhen, Gen, Xun, and Dui</term> combine to form the sixty-four hexagrams.

They are roused by thunder and lightning; these are the images of **The Arousing** and **Keeping Still**. <note>original: 震 (Zhen) and 艮 (根). In this context, the author associates these trigrams with the electrical energy of the atmosphere.</note> They are moistened by wind and rain; these are the images of **The Gentle** and **The Joyous**. <note>original: 巽 (Xun) and 兌 (Dui).</note> The sun and moon revolve and move; these are the images of **The Abysmal** and **The Clinging**. <note>original: 坎 (Kan) and 離 (Li); representing the lunar and solar cycles.</note>

The alternation of cold and heat occurs as the sun moves: when it moves south, it is at its furthest distance from the celestial pole, resulting in cold; when it moves north, it is at its closest point to the pole, resulting in heat. These phenomena are what constitute the "images" of transformation. The Way of **The Creative** forms the male, and the Way of **The Receptive** forms the female. These are what constitute the "physical forms" of transformation. If one examines the accompanying diagrams while reflecting on these principles, more than half of the meaning will be clear.

<summary>This page describes the dynamic interaction (rubbing and stirring) of the Eight Trigrams to form the sixty-four hexagrams and explains how these movements manifest as natural phenomena like weather, seasons, and gender.</summary>
<keywords>Eight Trigrams, Sixty-four Hexagrams, Qian and Kun, cosmology, seasonal cycles, atmospheric phenomena, formation of male and female</keywords>

# The Square Diagram of the Sixty-Four Hexagrams Produced by Doubling

> The *Exposition of the Trigrams* <note>original: 說卦 (Shuogua); one of the "Ten Wings" or ancient commentaries on the I Ching.</note> says: Thunder moves things; wind scatters them; rain moistens them; the sun warms them; Keeping Still stops them; the Joyous pleases them; the Creative rules them; the Receptive stores them.

The text above clarifies the symbolic imagery of the "Prior Heaven" <note>original: 先天 (Xiantian); refers to the primordial, ideal state of the universe before it enters the flow of time.</note> square diagram of the sixty-four hexagrams. Master Shao <note>Shao Yong (1011–1077), a cosmologist famous for his mathematical approach to the I Ching.</note> stated: "All diagrams begin from the center." 

By observing the diagram now, we see that "Thunder moves things" <term>The trigram Zhen (震); representing the Arousing force.</term> and "Wind scatters them" <term>The trigram Xun (巽); representing the Gentle or penetrating force.</term> are positioned exactly at the center of this diagram and within the "four dimensions" <note>The four diagonal corners.</note>. 

Next, regarding "Rain moistens them" and "the sun warms them," the Abysmal <term>Kan (坎); the element of Water.</term> follows the Gentle (Xun), while the Clinging <term>Li (離); the element of Fire.</term> follows the Arousing (Zhen). 

Regarding "Keeping Still stops them" and "the Joyous pleases them," Keeping Still <term>Gen (艮); the element of Mountain.</term> follows the Abysmal (Kan), while the Joyous <term>Dui (兌); the element of Lake.</term> follows the Clinging (Li). 

Finally, regarding "the Creative rules them" and "the Receptive stores them," the Creative <term>Qian (乾); representing Heaven.</term> follows the Joyous (Dui), and the Receptive <term>Kun (坤); representing Earth.</term> follows Keeping Still (Gen). 

All these sequences originate from the center and reach outward to the exterior; for this reason, they move ->left and right, upward and downward.<-

<meta>This page provides a technical breakdown of the "Square Diagram," explaining how the vertical and horizontal axes align the sixty-four hexagrams with the solar calendar (the twelve months) and the compass points (represented by the Earthly Branches).</meta>

### Gold Casket <unclear>Collection</unclear> — Complete Library in the Four Treasuries

**Middle Volume**

Through the intersection of vertical and horizontal lines, the positions of the sixty-four hexagrams are determined. The southeast is the Yang direction, and the Creative <term>Qian (乾); the hexagram representing pure Heaven and active energy.</term> occupies it. The Creative is the hexagram associated with the fourth month; therefore, it is positioned at the branch of the Serpent <note>original: 巳 (Si); the sixth Earthly Branch, corresponding to the transition from spring to summer and the southeast direction.</note>. The northwest is the Yin direction, and the Receptive <term>Kun (坤); the hexagram representing pure Earth and receptive energy.</term> occupies it. The Receptive is the hexagram of the tenth month; therefore, it is positioned at the branch of the Boar <note>original: 亥 (Hai); the twelfth Earthly Branch, corresponding to the transition into late winter and the northwest direction.</note>.

Peace <term>Tai (泰); Hexagram 11, symbolizing harmony between Heaven and Earth.</term> is the hexagram of the first month and is positioned at the branch of the Tiger <note>original: 寅 (Yin); the third Earthly Branch, marking the beginning of Spring.</note>. Obstruction <term>Pi (否); Hexagram 12, symbolizing the separation of Heaven and Earth.</term> is the hexagram of the seventh month and is positioned at the branch of the Monkey <note>original: 申 (Shen); the ninth Earthly Branch, marking the beginning of Autumn.</note>. These four—the Tiger, Monkey, Serpent, and Boar—constitute the positions of the "four corners" <note>The diagonal directions of the compass.</note>.

At the midpoint of the Winter Solstice, within the branch of the Rat <note>original: 子 (Zi); representing the North and the dead of winter.</note>, a single Yang line is born, and the hexagram Return <term>Fu (復); Hexagram 24, signifying the return of light.</term> governs this moment. Return is the hexagram of the eleventh month. At the midpoint of the Summer Solstice, within the branch of the Horse <note>original: 午 (Wu); representing the South and the height of summer.</note>, a single Yin line is born, and the hexagram Coming to Meet <term>Gou (姤); Hexagram 44, signifying the first shadow of darkness.</term> governs this moment. Coming to Meet is the hexagram of the fifth month.

At the midpoint of the Spring Equinox, within the branch of the Rabbit <note>original: 卯 (Mao); representing the East.</note>, the hexagram Power of the Great <term>Da Zhuang (大壯); Hexagram 34.</term> corresponds to the second month. At the midpoint of the Autumn Equinox, within the branch of the Rooster <note>original: 酉 (You); representing the West.</note>, the hexagram Contemplation <term>Guan (觀); Hexagram 20.</term> corresponds to the eighth month. These four—the Rat, Horse, Rabbit, and Rooster—constitute the "four cardinal" positions <note>The primary directions of North, South, East, and West.</note>. The hexagrams for the months of the Dragon, Dog, Ox, and Goat <note>original: 辰戌丑未 (Chen, Xu, Chou, Wei); the remaining four Earthly Branches.</note> are situated in offset positions...

Furthermore, if one views the system with the Creative at the top and the Receptive at the bottom, then the eight fundamental trigrams serve as the "warp" <note>The vertical threads in weaving.</note> while the fifty-six hexagrams to the left and right serve as the "weft" <note>The horizontal threads in weaving.</note>. This is what the Commentary refers to as: "Heaven and Earth established the positions, and the *Changes* <note>The *I Ching* or the cosmic process of transformation.</note> operate within them."

If one views it with Obstruction at the top and Peace at the bottom, then the eight trigrams form the primary ranks, and the fifty-six hexagrams intersect above and below. # Imperial Edition of the Complete Library of the Four Treasuries
## Volume Two

The twenty-eight hexagrams correspond above to the twenty-eight lunar mansions <note>Traditional divisions of the sky used in ancient Chinese astronomy to mark the moon's progress across the heavens.</note> of the sky. From the Creative <term>Qian (乾); the hexagram representing pure Heaven and masculine energy.</term> to Great Accumulation <term>Da Chu (大畜); the hexagram representing the storage of great potential.</term>, there are seven hexagrams in total.

These seven correspond above to the seven mansions of the Azure Dragon of the East. The Horn mansion <note>original: "Jiao" (角)</note> begins with the Creative; thus, the earthly branch of the Dragon <note>original: "Chen" (辰)</note> corresponds to the Neck Golden Dragon mansion, and the branch of the Rabbit <note>original: "Mao" (卯)</note> corresponds to the Room Sun Rabbit mansion.

From Peace <term>Tai (泰); the hexagram representing harmony and the first lunar month.</term> to Modesty <term>Qian (謙); the hexagram representing humility and inner strength.</term>, there are seven hexagrams in total, which correspond to the seven mansions of the Black Tortoise <note>The "Black Warrior," the symbol of the North and the winter season.</note> of the North. Here, the earthly branch of the Ox <note>original: "Chou" (丑)</note> corresponds to the Ox Golden Ox mansion, and the branch of the Rat <note>original: "Zi" (子)</note> corresponds to the Void Sun Rat mansion.

From the Receptive <term>Kun (坤); the hexagram representing pure Earth and feminine devotion.</term> to Gathering Together <term>Cui (萃); the hexagram representing the assembly of people or things.</term>, there are seven hexagrams in total, which correspond to the seven mansions of the White Tiger of the West. The earthly branch of the Dog <note>original: "Xu" (戌)</note> corresponds to the Bond Golden Dog mansion, and the branch of the Rooster <note>original: "You" (酉)</note> corresponds to the Hairy Head Sun Rooster mansion <note>Often identified with the Pleiades star cluster.</note>.

From Obstruction <term>Pi (否); the hexagram representing stagnation or the seventh lunar month.</term> to Treading <term>Lu (履); the hexagram representing conduct and cautious progress.</term>, there are seven hexagrams in total, which correspond to the seven mansions of the Vermilion Bird of the South. The earthly branch of the Goat <note>original: "未" (Wei)</note> corresponds to the Ghost Golden Sheep mansion, and the branch of the Horse <note>original: "午" (Wu)</note> corresponds to the Star Sun Horse mansion.

Therefore it is said: "In the heavens, images are formed; on the earth, shapes are formed" <note>A famous quote from the *Classic of Changes* suggesting that the physical world is a mirror of celestial movements.</note>. Furthermore, the Commentary on the Decision <term>Tuan (彖); the portion of the text that explains the core meaning of a hexagram's judgment.</term> for the hexagram Grace <term>Bi (賁); the hexagram representing elegance, ornament, and the patterns of nature.</term> says: "Observe the patterns of the heavens to examine the changes of the seasons; observe the patterns of humanity to transform and perfect the world."

<summary>This page explains the correlation between twenty-eight specific hexagrams of the I Ching and the twenty-eight lunar mansions of ancient Chinese astronomy, organized by the four cardinal directions and their symbolic animals. It concludes by emphasizing that understanding these celestial patterns allows one to understand the changes of time and the cultivation of human society.</summary>
<keywords>Twenty-eight Lunar Mansions, Azure Dragon, Black Tortoise, White Tiger, Vermilion Bird, Qian (Creative), Kun (Receptive), Bi (Grace), celestial patterns, earthly branches</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the celestial mapping of hexagrams to the twenty-eight lunar mansions into a specific commentary based on the *Kinship of the Three* (Cantong Qi). It focuses on the "Sovereign Hexagrams" (the twelve hexagrams that represent the months) and how they track the movement of vital energy through the seasons and musical pitch-pipes.</meta>

<margin>
Inner Chapters of the Illustrated Explanation of the Changes' Imagery
</margin>

The *Kinship of the Three* <note>original: "Zhouyi Cantong Qi" (周易參同契); an ancient text on alchemy and the I Ching, often called the "ancestor of all alchemical books."</note> says: At the dawn of the new moon, the hexagram is **Return** <term>Fu (復); representing the first return of light and the winter solstice.</term>. The vital energy <note>original: "Yang Qi" (陽氣)</note> begins to flow; its coming and going is without distress.

Setting the gnomon <note>A vertical pillar used to measure the sun's shadow for calendrical purposes.</note>, the strength is subtle. The **Yellow Bell** pitch-pipe <term>Huang Zhong (黃鍾); the first and longest of the twelve standard pitch-pipes, corresponding to the winter solstice.</term> is established in the month of the Rat <note>original: "Zi" (子), the eleventh month of the lunar calendar.</note>. The omens then flourish and manifest; spreading gentle warmth, the common people find their stability.

**Approach** <term>Lin (臨); representing the growth of Yang and the twelfth lunar month.</term> acts as the furnace to spread the branches, opening the path for upright light. The radiance gradually advances, and the days grow increasingly longer in the month of the Ox <note>original: "Chou" (丑)</note>.

The **Great Tubule** pitch-pipe <term>Da Lü (大呂); the second pitch-pipe, corresponding to the transition toward spring.</term> regulates the rise and fall of energy. Looking up, we see the completion of **Peace** <term>Tai (泰); the hexagram where Heaven and Earth meet in harmony, corresponding to the first lunar month.</term>. Strength and yielding are both at their peak; the Yin and Yang forces intermingle.

"The small departs and the great arrives" <note>A famous phrase from the I Ching describing the hexagram Tai, where negative influences leave and positive ones return.</note> as the spokes converge in the month of the Tiger <note>original: "Yin" (寅)</note>. Moving with the seasons, we gradually pass into **Great Strength** <term>Da Zhuang (大壯); representing the second lunar month when Yang energy is powerful.</term>, which brings order to the ranks.

At the gate of the Rabbit <note>original: "Mao" (卯), the third lunar month and the time of the spring equinox.</note>, the seeds of the elm tree fall and return to their roots. Punishment and virtue <note>original: "Xing-De" (刑德); a cosmological concept where Yin represents destructive or punitive forces and Yang represents creative or virtuous forces.</note> balance each other; day and night are now equal in length.

At **Breakthrough** <term>Guai (夬); the third lunar month, where Yang is about to displace the last of the Yin.</term>, Yin retreats and Yang ascends forward. It is a time to wash the wings and shake off the old dust. Then comes **The Creative** <term>Qian (乾); the hexagram of pure Yang, corresponding to the fourth lunar month.</term>, vigorous and strong.

Its abundant brilliance spreads to all neighbors. The Yang cycle reaches its fullness in the month of the Snake <note>original: "Si" (巳)</note>. Having reached the end, a transition occurs; **Coming to Meet** <term>Gou (姤); the fifth lunar month and the summer solstice, where a single line of Yin returns.</term> begins the new thread of the cycle.

<summary>This page uses the "Kinship of the Three" to explain the seasonal progression of the Sovereign Hexagrams, linking them to specific months, earthly branches, and musical pitch-pipes. It describes the growth of Yang energy from the winter solstice through the spring equinox to the height of summer.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the sequence of the twelve "Sovereign Hexagrams" begun on the previous page. Having reached the peak of light (Yang), the text now describes the descent into darkness (Yin) through the summer and autumn months, leading back to the primal state of the Earth.</meta>

### Volume Two

<margin>
Book Two
</margin>

"Treading on hoarfrost" <note>original: "lü shuang" (履霜); a reference to the first line of the I Ching’s second hexagram, warning that when you feel frost underfoot, hard ice is not far off.</note> begins first as the cold spring at the bottom of the well. The solar month of the Horse <note>original: "Wu" (午); the fifth lunar month, containing the Summer Solstice.</note> corresponds to the **Luxuriant Guest** pitch-pipe <term>Rui Bin (蕤賓); the seventh pitch-pipe, marking the point where Yin energy "guests" within the dominant Yang.</term>. The guest submits to the Yin energy, and Yin becomes the master of the house.

**Withdrawal** <note>Hexagram 33: Dun (遯); representing the sixth lunar month.</note>
Leaves its position in the world to gather its essence. It harbors virtue and awaits the proper time, dwelling in a state of obscure retreat.

**Obstruction** <note>Hexagram 12: Pi (否); representing the seventh lunar month.</note>
A state of being closed and disconnected; those things meant to sprout do not grow. As Yin expands, Yang is forced to contract, until even the "name and surname" <note>A metaphor for the visible identity or manifest power of the sun/Yang.</note> of Yang are submerged.

**Contemplation** <note>Hexagram 20: Guan (觀); representing the eighth lunar month.</note>
One observes the brilliance of the landscape and examines the character of mid-autumn. It takes on the duty of nourishing the tiny and the young; even as the old and withered fall, others flourish again. During this time, the shepherd's purse and the wheat sprout and bud, prospering together as they are born.

**Stripping Away** <note>Hexagram 23: Bo (剝); representing the ninth lunar month.</note>
The "limbs and body" of the year rot away, and the physical form is extinguished. Once the transformative energy <note>original: "Hua Qi" (化氣)</note> is exhausted, the supreme spirit is lost.

When the Way reaches its limit, it returns to the **Primal Earth** <note>Hexagram 02: Kun (坤); representing the tenth lunar month.</note>.
The Primal Earth <term>Kun Yuan (坤元); the fundamental creative capacity of the Earth/Yin principle.</term> is eternally submissive. Following the principles of the Earth, it receives the influence of Heaven to spread and declare its power through the dark and hidden realms.

Though far, distant, and separated by great voids, all things remain connected. Responding to the celestial measures to nurture the seeds of life, this is the source of Yin and Yang. In the vast and indistinct void, none can perceive its beginning. At first, one may be confused and lose the track, but later this state becomes the sovereign ruler. No plain is without a slope <note>original: "wu ping bu bei" (無平不陂); a classic proverb from the I Ching meaning that every period of ease is followed by difficulty, and every departure by a return.</note>; this is the natural spontaneity of the Way. Through change and transformation, the cycle becomes ever more vigorous.

<summary>This page describes the waning of Yang energy through the hexagrams of late summer and autumn, culminating in the total Yin of the Kun hexagram, which serves as the hidden source for future rebirth.</summary>

<keywords>Sovereign Hexagrams, Yin and Yang, Rui Bin pitch-pipe, mid-autumn, Kun Yuan, cycle of seasons, alchemical transformation</keywords>

# Inner Chapters of Illustrations and Explanations of I Ching Imagery

->The waning and waxing of energies follow one another; ending with **The Receptive** <note>Hexagram 2: Kun</note> and beginning again with **Return** <note>Hexagram 24: Fu</note>, the cycle is like a continuous ring. Emperors and kings who govern by this principle shall endure for a thousand years.<-

**Master Shao’s** <note>Shao Yong (1011–1077), a famous cosmologist of the Song Dynasty.</note> **Song of the Great Yi says:**

*Heaven and Earth determine positions,*
*Obstruction and Peace are of opposite types.*
*Mountain and Marsh communicate their breath,*
*Loss and Influence reveal their meaning.*
*Thunder and Wind buffet each other,*
*Duration and Increase give rise to intent.*
*Water and Fire shoot at each other,*
*Already Fulfilled and Not Yet Fulfilled.*

The **Four Images** <term>Si Xiang (四象); the four stages of Yin and Yang (Great Yang, Lesser Yin, Lesser Yang, Great Yin)</term> interact to form sixteen matters; the **Eight Trigrams** <term>Ba Gua (八卦); the fundamental three-line symbols of the I Ching</term> double themselves to become the sixty-four hexagrams. **Master Zhu** <note>Zhu Xi (1130–1200), the most influential Neo-Confucian scholar.</note> says this explains the two intersecting diagonal lines of the **Square Map** <term>Fang Tu (方圖); a grid arrangement of the 64 hexagrams attributed to Shao Yong</term>. At the southeast corner is **The Creative** <note>Hexagram 1: Qian (Heaven)</note>, which then directly faces **The Receptive** <note>Hexagram 2: Kun (Earth)</note> at the northwest corner. <gloss>Older versions noted the southeast corner as The Receptive and the northwest as The Creative.</gloss>

This is what is meant by "Heaven and Earth determining positions." In the southwest corner is **Obstruction** <note>Hexagram 12: Pi</note>, which faces **Peace** <note>Hexagram 11: Tai</note> in the northeast corner. Thus, "Obstruction and Peace are of opposite types." One cannot fathom how such a design could be so ingenious!

**Master Cheng Zhifang of Xin’an** <note>A Yuan-dynasty scholar and commentator on the works of Shao Yong.</note> says: "Master Shao called the **Study of Pre-Heaven** <term>Xian Tian Xue (先天學); the study of the cosmic order as it existed before the physical world was formed</term> a 'Method of the Mind.' The diagrams all begin from the center; the ten thousand transformations and all worldly matters are born from the heart." He says:

# Imperial Edition of the Four Treasuries, Middle Volume

The word "both" refers to considering the **Circular** and **Square Maps** together. The phrase "Heaven and Earth determine positions" <note>A reference to the *Shuo Gua* commentary, signifying the axis of the universe</note> indicates that the **Circular Map** <term>Yuan Tu (圓圖); a circular arrangement representing the celestial cycle of time</term> originates from the center. The phrase "Thunder moves and Wind disperses" indicates that the **Square Map** <term>Fang Tu (方圖); a grid arrangement representing the terrestrial stability of space</term> also originates from the center. The Circular represents movement, taking the "determination of positions" as its foundation; the Square represents stillness, taking "movement and dispersal" as its application. To move yet remain unmoved, and to be still yet not remain still—this is truly the "Method of the Heart" of the **Pre-Celestial** <term>Xian Tian (先天); the "Before Heaven" or primordial state of the universe</term> state.

> The Commentary says: The **Eight Trigrams** <term>Ba Gua (八卦); the eight fundamental three-line symbols</term> being arranged in order, the images <note>symbolic representations of all phenomena</note> are contained within them. By doubling them <note>combining two trigrams to form a six-line hexagram</note>, the individual lines are contained within them. Hard and soft <note>Yang and Yin</note> push against one another, and change is contained within them. By appending remarks to name them, movement is contained within them. Good fortune, misfortune, remorse, and regret are those things born from movement.

The text above is the first section of the second part of the **Appended Remarks** <note>The *Xi Ci Zhuan*, a core philosophical commentary on the I Ching</note>. It clarifies the meaning of the **Pre-Celestial Sixty-Four Hexagrams Square Map**. The phrase "the eight trigrams are arranged in order" refers to the sequence of **The Creative** (Qian), **The Joyous** (Dui), **The Clinging** (Li), **The Arousing** (Zhen), **The Gentle** (Xun), **The Abysmal** (Kan), **The Keeping Still** (Gen), and **The Receptive** (Kun) forming rows and columns. This sequence is counted starting from the top.

<summary>This page explains the metaphysical relationship between the Circular and Square maps of the I Ching, identifying the Circular with movement (time) and the Square with stillness (space). It uses the "Appended Remarks" commentary to validate the transition from eight basic trigrams to the sixty-four complex hexagrams.</summary>
<keywords>Circular Map, Square Map, Pre-Celestial (Xian Tian), Appended Remarks (Xi Ci), Eight Trigrams, hexagram structure, movement and stillness</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical geometric explanation of the Pre-Celestial (Xian Tian) diagrams, specifically describing how the Eight Trigrams are sequenced within the Square Map and how they are "doubled" to form the sixty-four hexagrams through the movement of Yin and Yang lines.</meta>

<margin>
Inner Chapter of the *Illustrated Explanation of the Imagery of the Yi*
</margin>

Counting downwards from left to right, or counting from the center outward, the sequence of **Zhen** <term>Thunder</term>, **Li** <term>Fire</term>, **Dui** <term>Lake</term>, **Qian** <term>Heaven</term>, **Xun** <term>Wind</term>, **Kan** <term>Water</term>, **Gen** <term>Mountain</term>, and **Kun** <term>Earth</term> also forms a structured array. Whether one looks horizontally, diagonally, curved, straight, left, right, front, or back, this order is never not present. Within this structure, the cosmic images of Heaven, Earth, Water, Fire, Thunder, Wind, Mountain, and Marsh are all contained. 

The phrase "doubling them" <note>referring to the process of stacking two trigrams to create a six-line hexagram</note> means that atop each of the **Eight Trigrams**, another eight trigrams are added. By distinguishing between **Yin and Yang** and alternating the use of **Soft and Hard** <note>Yin and Yang lines</note>, the six positions of the lines—from the first, second, third, fourth, and fifth, up to the top—are established within them. 

As for the "pushing of the hard and soft against one another": the Hard <note>Yang</note> arises from the "First Nine" <note>The first Yang line</note> of the hexagram **Fu** <term>Return; Hexagram 24, representing the first return of light/Yang</term>. From the first line, it is pushed upward through the second, third, fourth, and fifth positions in order, until it reaches the six lines of **Qian** <term>Heaven; the hexagram of pure Yang</term>. The Soft <note>Yin</note> arises from the "First Six" <note>The first Yin line</note> of the hexagram **Gou** <term>Encounter; Hexagram 44, where the first shadow/Yin appears</term>. From the first line, it is pushed downward <note>meaning it begins to displace the Yang lines from the bottom up as the cycle progresses</note> through the second, third, fourth, and fifth positions until it reaches the six lines of **Kun** <term>Earth; the hexagram of pure Yin</term>. Thus, the transformations of the hexagrams and their individual lines are all contained within this system. The Sage, by means of these hexagrams and lines...

<summary>This page explains the geometric and mathematical logic of the I Ching's Square Map, detailing how trigrams are sequenced and "doubled" into hexagrams. It describes the cyclical "pushing" of Yin and Yang lines through the pivotal hexagrams of Return (Fu) and Encounter (Gou).</summary>

<meta>This page continues the exploration of the sixty-four hexagrams, moving from the visual diagram seen on the previous page to a theoretical explanation of how the "Hard" (Yang) and "Soft" (Yin) lines interact to create change. # Illustrated Explanation of the Imagery of the Changes, Inner Chapters

The commentary to the right <note>In traditional Chinese layout, "to the right" refers to the text or diagram that preceded this section, as the script is read from right to left.</note> elucidates the meaning of the **transformation and communication** <note>original: "變通" (biàntōng) — a key concept in the I Ching referring to how things adapt and flow through change.</note> of the sixty-four hexagrams in the **Later Heaven** <note>original: "後天" (hòutiān) — the sequence of hexagrams attributed to King Wen, representing the physical world and the cycle of time.</note> arrangement. **Hardness and Softness** <note>original: "剛柔" (gāngróu) — these terms represent Yang (the solid line) and Yin (the broken line) respectively.</note> constitute the fundamental essence of this transformation, while the transformation itself is the timely application of these forces.

By deducing from the diagram, we see that **Heaven** (*Qian*) is hard and **Earth** (*Kun*) is soft, positioned respectively above and below. This is their fixed and unchanging form; therefore, it is said that "hardness and softness are that which establish the foundation." When the first line of the Earth hexagram (which is soft) transforms and trends toward hardness, it creates the hexagrams of **Return** (*Fu*), **Approach** (*Lin*), **Peace** (*Tai*), **Great Strength** (*Da Zhuang*), and **Breakthrough** (*Guai*), as the energy advances upward.

<column-break/>

# The Diagram of the Transformation and Communication of the Sixty-Four Hexagrams

> The Commentary <note>Referring to the *Xici Zhuan*, or "The Great Commentary," one of the most important philosophical appendices to the I Ching.</note> says: "Hardness and softness are that which establish the foundation. Transformation and communication are that which follow the seasons. Good and bad fortune are determined by **steadfastness** <note>original: "貞" (zhēn) — often translated as "constancy" or "divination," here implying a moral persistence that overcomes adversity.</note>. The Way of Heaven and Earth is revealed through steadfast contemplation. The Way of the Sun and Moon is revealed through steadfast brilliance. The movements under Heaven are made steadfast through oneness."

<summary>This page explains the metaphysical relationship between the fixed states of Yin and Yang and the dynamic process of seasonal change. It uses the progression of specific hexagrams to illustrate how "Hardness" (Yang) gradually displaces "Softness" (Yin) to create the cycle of the year.</summary>
<keywords>Hard and Soft, Yang and Yin, Later Heaven sequence, transformation and communication, steadfastness, Qian and Kun, hexagram progression</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the theoretical explanation from the previous page. While the previous section described the progression from Earth (Kun) to Heaven (Qian) as an upward advancement of energy, this text describes the reverse process—the descent from summer to winter—and provides a mathematical breakdown of the resulting hexagram permutations based on how many lines (Yao) change.</meta>

### Imperially Commissioned Complete Library of the Four Treasuries, Volume II

Reaching the extreme, it becomes **Heaven** <note>original: "乾" (Qián) — representing the pure active, creative, and masculine principle (Yang).</note> as the cycle moves from winter to summer. When the first line <note>original: "爻" (yáo) — the individual horizontal lines that make up a hexagram.</note> of **Heaven** is Hard <note>referring to a solid Yang line</note> but transforms and trends toward the Soft <note>referring to a broken Yin line</note>, it creates the hexagrams of **Meeting** <note>original: "姤" (Gòu)</note>, **Retreat** <note>original: "遯" (Dùn)</note>, **Obstruction** <note>original: "否" (Pǐ)</note>, **Observation** <note>original: "觀" (Guān)</note>, and **Stripping Away** <note>original: "剝" (Bō)</note>.

Reaching the extreme of this retreat, it becomes **Earth** <note>original: "坤" (Kūn) — representing the pure receptive, yielding, and feminine principle (Yin).</note> as the cycle moves from summer to winter. Therefore, it is said that **transformation and communication** <note>original: "變通" (biàntōng) — the ability to adapt and flow with the changing world.</note> are that which **follow the timing of the seasons** <note>original: "趨時" (qūshí) — the principle of acting in harmony with the current moment.</note>.

Thus, regarding the primary hexagrams of **Heaven and Earth**, when only the first line changes, there are six hexagrams each containing either one Yin or one Yang line; these are all deduced by following the logic of **Return** <note>original: "復" (Fù)</note> and **Meeting**. When two lines change, there are fifteen hexagrams each containing two Yin or two Yang lines; these are all deduced from **Approach** <note>original: "臨" (Lín)</note> and **Retreat**. When three lines change, there are twenty hexagrams each containing three Yin or three Yang lines; these are all deduced from **Peace** <note>original: "泰" (Tài)</note> and **Obstruction**. When four lines change, there are fifteen hexagrams each containing four Yin or four Yang lines; these are all deduced from **Great Strength** <note>original: "大壯" (Dà Zhuàng)</note> and **Observation**. When five lines change, there are six hexagrams each containing five Yin or five Yang lines; these are all deduced from **Breakthrough** <note>original: "夬" (Guài)</note> and **Stripping Away**. Whether viewed vertically or horizontally, moving upward or downward, or in reversal and repetition...

<summary>This page explains the seasonal descent from the peak of Yang (Summer/Heaven) back to Yin (Winter/Earth) and categorizes the sixty-four hexagrams based on the number of changing lines relative to the primary hexagrams of Heaven and Earth.</summary>

<keywords>I Ching, hexagram transformation, Qian and Kun, seasonal cycles, Yang and Yin lines, mathematical permutations, Siku Quanshu</keywords>

### Illustrated Explanation of the Diagrams of the Changes: Inner Chapters

->Page Three<-

The mutual pushing and pulling [of these forces] is applicable everywhere. The key lies in recognizing their **thorough-transformation** <note>original: "通變" (tōngbiàn) — the ability of cosmic principles to adapt and flow through all circumstances without becoming stuck.</note>; once this is understood, one is no longer bound by rigid rules, and everything becomes interconnected.

As the *Commentary* <note>referring to the *Xici* or "Great Treatise," a foundational philosophical commentary on the *I Ching*.</note> says: "Movement and change do not remain in one place; they circulate through the **six voids** <note>original: "六虛" (liùxū) — the six empty "slots" or positions within a hexagram that the lines move through.</note>. Rising and falling without constancy, the **Hard and Soft** <note>referring to the solid Yang lines and broken Yin lines.</note> transform into one another. They cannot be taken as a fixed rule; it is only change that is followed."

Indeed, the **Yang** principle governs advancement, ascending to the left starting from the hexagram **Return** <note>original: "復" (Fù) — representing the first return of light/Yang at the winter solstice.</note>. The **Yin** principle governs retreat, descending to the right starting from the hexagram **Meeting** <note>original: "姤" (Gòu) — representing the first arrival of darkness/Yin at the summer solstice.</note>.

In the hexagrams of **Peace** <note>original: "泰" (Tài) — where Yang is below and Yin is above, moving toward each other.</note> and **Obstruction** <note>original: "否" (Pǐ) — where the forces move away from each other.</note>, Yin and Yang are divided equally in the middle. From the hour of the **Tiger** to the hour of the **Monkey** <note>from approximately 3:00 AM to 5:00 PM.</note>, all is daytime, and **Heaven** <note>original: "乾" (Qián)</note> truly permeates this period. From the hour of the **Rooster** to the hour of the **Ox** <note>from approximately 5:00 PM to 3:00 AM.</note>, all is nighttime, and **Earth** <note>original: "坤" (Kūn)</note> truly sustains it. 

Therefore, the first part of the *Appended Phrases* states: "Transformation and change are the images of advancement and retreat; hardness and softness are the images of day and night." Whether through hardness or softness, there is loss and gain, and the principles of good and bad fortune constantly contend with one another. 

With **Heaven** above and **Earth** below, the fixed structure does not change; this is what is known as the **steadfast observation** <note>original: "貞觀" (zhēnguàn) — the way the universe reveals itself through its unwavering, natural regularity.</note> of the Way of Heaven and Earth. With **Fire** <note>original: "離" (Lí) — associated with the Sun and the South.</note> in the South and **Water** <note>original: "坎" (Kǎn) — associated with the Moon and the North.</note> in the North, the Sun is beautiful in the day, while the Moon is manifest in the...

<summary>This page explains that the I Ching's hexagrams are not rigid rules but models of fluid change, mapping the transition of Yang and Yin onto the solar cycle and the hours of the day. It emphasizes that Heaven permeates the day while Earth sustains the night, illustrating the "steadfast observation" of natural law.</summary>
<keywords>通變 (thorough-transformation), 六虛 (six voids), 乾坤 (Heaven and Earth), 離坎 (Fire and Water), 貞觀 (Steadfast Observation), Circadian cycle, Great Treatise</keywords>

# Complete Books of the Four Treasuries of the Golden Elixir: Volume II

->Page Four<-

The Way of the Sun and Moon is that of pure brilliance. In the movements of the world, changes are infinite. Following the natural Principle leads to good fortune, while defying it leads to misfortune. That which is upright and constant remains unified through **Perseverance** <note>original: "恆" (héng) — this refers to both the concept of constancy and the 32nd hexagram, which represents the enduring nature of cosmic laws.</note>; this is ultimately but a single Principle.

According to Master Zhu <note>referring to Zhu Xi (1130–1200), the preeminent Neo-Confucian scholar who wrote a famous commentary on the *Cantong qi* under a pseudonym.</note>, the *Kinship of the Three* <note>original: *Cantong qi* — the "Ancestor of Alchemical Texts," which uses the *I Ching* to explain the secrets of longevity and spiritual transformation.</note> treats the hexagrams **Heaven and Earth** <note>original: "乾坤" (*Qián* and *Kūn*).</note> as the **cauldron and vessel** <note>the metaphorical "laboratory equipment" of the body; the container in which the elixirs are refined.</note>, and the hexagrams **Water and Fire** <note>original: "坎離" (*Kǎn* and *Lí*).</note> as the **ingredients** <note>original: "藥物" (*yàowù*) — the essential medicinal substances or internal energies to be processed.</note>. The remaining sixty hexagrams represent the **fire timing** <note>original: "火候" (*huǒhòu*) — the precise schedule and intensity of heat (meditative focus) used during the alchemical process.</note>.

If we now deduce the meaning from this diagram, it essentially aligns the form of the human body with the Yin and Yang of Heaven and Earth. **Heaven** constitutes the head and resides above, while **Earth** constitutes the abdomen and resides below. **Fire** represents the heart, and **Water** represents the kidneys. Because the heart is fire and the kidneys are water, **Fire** is positioned above and **Water** is positioned below.

**Yang** arises from the hexagram **Return** <note>original: "復" (*Fù*) — representing the first spark of light/energy at the winter solstice.</note> and ascends from the left side. In the human body, this corresponds to the **Governing Vessel** <gloss>the Yang meridian</gloss>, which begins at the tailbone <note>original: "尻" (*kāo*) — the coccyx or "end of the spine."</note>, follows the spine upward, and travels to the head.

### Illustrated Explanations of the Hexagram Diagrams: Inner Chapters

->Page Four<-

Yin begins at the hexagram **Coming to Meet** <note>original: "姤" (gòu) — Hexagram 44, representing the first moment of Yin's return after the peak of Yang.</note>, descending from the right side of the body through the human **Conception Vessel** <note>original: "任脈" (rènmài)</note> <gloss>the Yin meridian</gloss>, starting from the throat, following the chest and stomach, and moving down toward the abdomen. 

The upper twenty hexagrams model **Heaven**. Heaven represents the "light and clear" aspect of Yang energy; therefore, these are all hexagrams composed of four or five Yang lines. 

The lower twenty hexagrams model **Earth**. Earth represents the "heavy and turbid" aspect of Yin energy; therefore, these are all hexagrams composed of four or five Yin lines. 

The middle twenty hexagrams symbolize **Humanity**. Humanity embodies the virtue of both Heaven and Earth and serves as the intersection of Yin and Yang; therefore, these are all hexagrams composed of three Yin and three Yang lines. This is exactly like the meridians of the human body, where the hands and feet each possess three Yin and three Yang channels. 

Furthermore, the principle holds that the upper part of the human body models Heaven, the middle part models Humanity, and the lower part models Earth. Speaking from this perspective, the meanings within the *Kinship of the Three* <note>original: *Cantong qi*</note> are indeed not false. 

As for the hexagram **Perseverance** <note>original: "恆" (héng) — Hexagram 32, representing enduring constancy.</note> occupying the center, this is what the *Book of Documents* calls "possessing a constant nature," what the *Commentaries* call "persevering with a single virtue," and what Mencius...

# Diagram of the Application of the Sixty-Four Hexagrams to the Right

The *Discussion of the Trigrams* <note>original: "説卦" (Shuō Guà), one of the "Ten Wings" or ancient commentaries on the I Ching</note> says:

> The Lord <note>referring to the creative force of nature or the divine</note> comes forth in the Arousing <note>East/Spring</note>; He brings all things to completion in the Gentle <note>Southeast</note>; He makes them manifest to one another in the Clinging <note>South/Summer</note>; He causes them to serve one another in the Receptive <note>Southwest</note>; He gives them joy in the Joyous <note>West/Autumn</note>; He battles in the Creative <note>Northwest</note>; He toils in the Abysmal <note>North/Winter</note>; He brings them to completion in the Keeping Still <note>Northeast</note>.

The text to the right clarifies the symbolism of the <term>Later Heaven</term> <note>the "Post-Celestial" arrangement attributed to King Wen, which describes the cycles of the manifest world and the seasons</note> diagram of the sixty-four hexagrams and their practical application. Regarding the origin of these hexagram positions, Master Zhu <note>the Song Dynasty philosopher Zhu Xi</note> once expressed doubt, suggesting that we should perhaps leave the matter as a mystery rather than forcing an explanation. Later scholars who attempted to explain it through the exchange of <term>hexagram lines</term> <note>original: "卦爻" (guà yáo), the individual broken and unbroken lines</note> or the sequence of the Five Elements were merely making subjective interpretations based on the existing positions; their views lack a solid foundation. 

Now, I have examined the transformations of the numbers in the <term>Yellow River Map</term> <note>original: "河圖" (Hé tú), a mythical cosmological diagram of dots representing numbers</note>, re-deduced the <term>Prior Heaven</term> <note>the "Pre-Celestial" arrangement attributed to Fu Xi, representing the ideal, timeless order</note> hexagram positions, and consulted various books on the tradition of <term>divination</term> <note>original: "卜筮" (bǔshì), the practice of seeking guidance through bones or stalks</note> to create a new diagram and unravel its...

### Complete Library of the Four Treasuries, Volume Lower

The explanation says: The reason <term>Qian</term> <note>The Creative; representing Heaven and the Father</note> begins in the Northwest is based on the principle that the "Heavens lean toward the Northwest." <note>This refers to an ancient Chinese cosmological myth where the sky tilted toward the Northwest after the pillar of heaven was broken, explaining the movement of celestial bodies.</note> <gloss>Originally missing</gloss>

->Since **Qian** is positioned in the Northwest, then **Kun** should be positioned—<-

—in the Southeast. However, because the earth is "not full" in the Southeast <note>a reference to the earth sinking in the Southeast, causing rivers to flow in that direction</note>, the trigram <term>Xun</term> <note>The Gentle/Wind; representing the Eldest Daughter</note> resides in that position instead. **Xun** is also the inverse of the <term>Pre-Celestial</term> <note>original: "先天" (Xiāntiān); the "Earlier Heaven" arrangement attributed to the mythical culture hero Fuxi</note> trigram **Dui**. For this reason, <term>Kun</term> <note>The Receptive; representing Earth and the Mother</note> retreats to reside in the Southwest. When all three <term>lines</term> <note>original: "爻" (yáo); the individual solid or broken strokes of a trigram</note> of **Kun** change, they become **Qian**; when all three lines of **Qian** change, they become **Kun**. They are diametrically opposed to one another, and thus the positions of **Qian** and **Kun** are established on a longitudinal axis. <term>Li</term> <note>The Clinging/Fire</note> flames upward and resides in the South, while <term>Kan</term> <note>The Abysmal/Water</note> moistens downward and resides in the North. If all three lines of **Kan** change, they become **Li**, and if all three lines of **Li** change, they become **Kan**. They are also diametrically opposed to one another, and thus the positions of **Kan** and **Li** are established on a longitudinal axis. <term>Gen</term> <note>Keeping Still/Mountain</note> is the inverse of the Pre-Celestial trigram **Zhen** <note>The Arousing/Thunder</note> and is positioned in the Northeast. **Xun** is the inverse of the Pre-Celestial **Dui** <note>The Joyous/Lake</note> and is positioned in the Southeast. **Gen**...

### Illustrated Explanation of the Imagery of the Changes: Inner Chapter

If all three <term>lines</term> <note>original: "爻" (yáo); the individual solid or broken strokes that make up a trigram</note> change, it becomes <term>Dui</term> <note>The Joyous/Lake; representing the Youngest Daughter</note>; however, when viewed from an inverted perspective, it is <term>Xun</term> <note>The Gentle/Wind; representing the Eldest Daughter</note>. If all three lines of **Xun** change, they become <term>Zhen</term> <note>The Arousing/Thunder; representing the Eldest Son</note>; viewed from an inverted perspective, it becomes <term>Gen</term> <note>Keeping Still/Mountain; representing the Youngest Son</note>. These are also mutually opposed, and thus the positions of **Gen** and **Xun** are established on a vertical axis. Lightning flashes and triggers thunder, therefore **Zhen** resides in the Due East, which was the position of **Li** in the <term>Pre-Celestial</term> <note>original: "先天" (Xiāntiān); the idealized cosmic arrangement of trigrams</note> arrangement. <gloss>The fire of **Li** flames upward, so its three lines change to **Zhen**</gloss>. Looking at the lines above, if all change, they become **Xun**; viewed from an inverted perspective, it becomes **Dui**. Water gathers to form a marsh, therefore **Dui** resides in the Due West, which was the position of <term>Kan</term> <note>The Abysmal/Water; representing the Middle Son</note> in the Pre-Celestial arrangement. <gloss>The water of **Kan** moistens downward, so its three lines change to **Dui**</gloss>. Looking from below, it is **Zhen**. Therefore, **Zhen** and **Dui** are opposed on the left and right, and their positions are established on a horizontal axis. 

<term>Master Shao</term> <note>Shao Yong (1011–1077 AD), a prominent Song Dynasty philosopher who deeply influenced the mathematical and cosmological interpretation of the I Ching</note> said: "**Zhen** and **Dui** are horizontal, while the other six trigrams are vertical—this is the practical application of the *Changes*." How profound these words are! It is precisely because of this that the "Palace Hexagrams" used in divination passed down to the present day also feature <term>Qian</term> and <term>Kun</term> as opposites, <term>Kan</term> and <term>Li</term> as opposites, and **Zhen** and **Dui**...

<summary>This page explains the geometric and symbolic logic behind the Later Heaven trigram map, emphasizing how the East-West horizontal axis (Zhen and Dui) relates to the movement of fire and water.</summary>
<keywords>Zhen, Dui, Master Shao, horizontal axis, vertical axis, line changes, Pre-Celestial arrangement, Later Heaven arrangement</keywords>

# Imperial Edition of the Complete Library of the Four Treasuries
## Illustrations and Descriptions of the Symbols of the Changes: Inner Chapters

The **Heng** hexagram <note>Duration; Hexagram 32</note> of the **Zhen** <note>The Arousing/Thunder</note> palace is the inversion of the **Xian** hexagram <note>Influence; Hexagram 31</note> of the **Dui** <note>The Joyous/Lake</note> palace. The **Sheng** hexagram <note>Pushing Upward; Hexagram 46</note> of **Zhen** is the inversion of the **Cui** hexagram <note>Gathering Together; Hexagram 45</note> of **Dui**. The **Jing** hexagram <note>The Well; Hexagram 48</note> of **Zhen** is the inversion of the **Kun** hexagram <note>Oppression/Exhaustion; Hexagram 47</note> of **Dui**.

As for the two categories known as **Wandering Soul** <term>You Hun; hexagrams where the "energy" or "soul" of the palace has drifted far from its original root</term> and **Returning Soul** <term>Gui Hun; hexagrams marking the point where the energy begins its journey back to the root</term> hexagrams, it is said that they do not follow the standard sequence of transformations within the Eight Palaces <note>The "Eight Palaces" is a system attributed to Jing Fang where the 64 hexagrams are grouped by their primary trigram</note>.

When the fifth line of the root palace hexagram changes, it determines the outer structure; if the three inner lines subsequently all change, it results in a **Wandering Soul** hexagram. It is called "**Wandering**" because the movement proceeds from the internal to the external.

If the fifth line changes but the three inner lines do not change, it results in a **Returning Soul** hexagram. It is called "**Returning**" because the movement reverts from the external back to the internal. 

Moving in cycles to the left and right, ascending and descending, rising and falling—the ritual laws and methods of a King <note>Referring to the "Li" or ceremonial structures that govern a harmonious society</note> are fully realized within these shifting patterns.

It suggests that these mathematical movements in the I Ching provide the cosmic blueprint for royal rituals and social order.</summary>

# Clarifying the Divination Stalks

<image-desc>A circular arrangement of fifty solid black dots representing the "Number of the Great Expansion." At the very center lies a single dot, surrounded by organized clusters that total fifty. This visualizes the mathematical totality of the universe before one stalk is "set aside" for the act of divination.</image-desc>

The **Commentary** <note>Referring to the *Xici* or "Great Treatise" of the *I Ching*</note> says: "The number of the **Great Expansion** <term>Da Yan; the process of expanding the simple numbers of the universe into the complexity of the 64 hexagrams</term> is fifty; of these, forty-nine are used."

**Master Zhu** <note>Zhu Xi (1130–1200), the influential Neo-Confucian scholar whose interpretations became the state standard</note> says: "The number of the **Great Expansion** is derived from the **River Map** <term>He Tu; a mythical cosmological diagram said to have appeared on the back of a dragon-horse</term>. It is obtained by taking the **Heavenly Five** <note>The OCR reads '天乂', likely a transcription error for '天五' (Heaven's Five), which sits at the center of the River Map</note> in the central palace and multiplying it by the **Earthly Ten**. Multiplying five by ten, or ten by five, both result in fifty.

When it comes to using them for **divination** <term>Shi; the ritual process of sorting yarrow stalks to determine hexagram lines</term>, one nonetheless stops at using forty-nine. This all arises from the natural necessity of **Principle** <term>Li; the fundamental order of the universe</term> and circumstances; it is not something that human intelligence can increase or decrease."

<summary>This page explains the cosmological origin of the number fifty, used in I Ching divination, by linking it to the mathematical symbols of the River Map. It justifies why one stalk is set aside, leaving forty-nine for actual use, as a reflection of natural law rather than human choice.</summary>
<keywords>Great Expansion, Da Yan, Zhu Xi, River Map, He Tu, yarrow stalk divination, Number Fifty, Neo-Confucianism</keywords>

--- Page 112 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical discussion of the yarrow stalks used in divination. While the previous page established the "Number of the Great Expansion" as fifty, this section explains the mathematical and symbolic logic behind why only forty-nine are used in practice, linking the count to the "virtue" of roundness and the structure of the trigrams.</meta>

### Volume Two

The forty-nine **yarrow stalks** <term>Shi; the dried stalks of the yarrow plant used to perform the ritual calculations of the I Ching</term> are gathered into a single bundle to model the image of the complete substance of the **Supreme Ultimate** <term>Taiji; the ultimate source and principle of all things in the universe</term>. The reason for this specific number is that it represents the sum of the actual lines that generate the **Eight Trigrams** <term>Bagua; the eight fundamental symbols representing the forces of the universe</term>.

The lines of the **Yang Symbol** <term>Yang Yi; the fundamental positive or active force</term> number seven <gloss>comprised of one line from the Two Symbols, two lines from the Four Images, and four lines from the Eight Trigrams</gloss>.

The lines of the **Yin Symbol** <term>Yin Yi; the fundamental negative or receptive force</term> also number seven <gloss>comprised of one line from the Two Symbols, two lines from the Four Images, and four lines from the Eight Trigrams</gloss>.

By multiplying these, we arrive at seven times seven, which is forty-nine.

The **Commentary** <note>Referring to the *Xici* or "Great Treatise" of the I Ching</note> says: "The **virtue** <term>De; the inherent nature or power of an object</term> of the stalks is round and divine." Roundness is associated with **odd numbers** <note>In ancient Chinese numerology, odd numbers are considered Yang/Heavenly, while even numbers are Yin/Earthly</note>. When seven "rounds" are gathered and clustered together, they form a natural circle.

The virtue of the hexagrams is square and wise. Squareness is associated with even numbers. When eight "squares" are aligned and stacked together, they form a natural square.

"Divine" refers to an unfathomable and wondrous application; "Wise" refers to a flexibility of transformation that is not restricted. Through the stalks (Yang), the hexagrams (Yin) are manifested; through movement, the hexagrams (Stillness) are revealed. The **substance and function** <note>Ti and Yong; a fundamental pair in Chinese philosophy where 'substance' is the internal reality and 'function' is its external manifestation</note> of the **Great Changes** <term>Da Yi; the I Ching as a cosmic system</term> reach their perfection here.

<summary>This page explains the numerology of the 49 yarrow stalks, deriving the number from the sum of the lines in the Two Symbols, Four Images, and Eight Trigrams (7x7). It contrasts the "round/divine" nature of the stalks with the "square/wise" nature of the resulting hexagrams.</summary>
<keywords>yarrow stalks, Supreme Ultimate, Eight Trigrams, Yang Symbol, Yin Symbol, numerology, substance and function, Great Changes</keywords>

# By Imperial Decree: The Complete Library of the Four Branches
## Illustrated Explanation of the *Book of Changes* Imagery, Inner Volume

### Page Ten

->Heaven<-  ->Earth<-

->The right hand divides them into one group<-

->In the middle, representing Man, one stalk is "hung"<-

->The left hand divides them into one group<-

The **Commentary** <note>Referring to the *Xici* or "Great Treatise" of the *I Ching*</note> says: "Divide them into two to represent the **Two Powers** <term>Liang; the fundamental pair of Heaven and Earth</term>, and hang one to represent the **Three Powers** <term>San; the cosmic triad of Heaven, Earth, and Humanity</term>."

The "Two" are **Heaven and Earth**. The "Three" is **Man**. To "hang" means to suspend or set aside. The *Yunhui* <note>An influential dictionary of the Yuan Dynasty</note> states: "It means to set something aside and not use it." 

Older interpretations claimed that the stalk was hung between the little finger and the ring finger. Because of this, the traditional teaching—"after the second **fingering** <term>le; the act of placing leftover stalks between the fingers during the counting process</term>, one then hangs the stalk"—becomes logically inconsistent. Some mistakenly believe this instruction means that one must hang a stalk during the "second change" <note>A "change" (bian) is one of the three rhythmic cycles of manipulation required to determine a single line of a hexagram</note>. If the definition of this single word "hang" is not clearly understood, the entire explanation of the ritual becomes hopelessly fragmented and confused.

<summary>This page explains the symbolic division of yarrow stalks, where two piles represent Heaven and Earth, and a single suspended stalk represents Humanity, completing the Cosmic Triad. The author criticts older interpretations that confused the physical placement of the "hung" stalk, arguing they lead to a fragmented understanding of the ritual.</summary>
<keywords>yarrow stalks, Heaven Earth and Man, Three Powers, divination ritual, Xici Commentary, hanging one, fingering</keywords>

Volume Two

Regarding the forty-nine **yarrow stalks** <term>shi; the dried stems of the yarrow plant used for I Ching divination</term>: divide them into two groups to represent the **Two Powers** <term>liangyi; the cosmic duality of Heaven and Earth</term>. Then, "hang" <note>original: "掛" (guà)</note> one stalk in the middle to represent **Humanity**. 

The remaining forty-eight stalks on the left and right represent the total substance of the **trigrams and lines** <term>gua yao</term>. The eight basic trigrams consist of twenty-four lines; when doubled <note>forming the sixty-four hexagrams</note>, they total forty-eight. Furthermore, as each of the eight trigrams undergoes eight transformations, their lines also total forty-eight. 

The **counting method** <term>shefa; the ritualized process of manipulating the stalks</term> is as follows: 

First, use the left hand to take the stalks from the left half and hold them. Then, use the right hand to take a single stalk from the right half and "hang" it in the middle. Do not move this stalk again. This represents **Humanity** dwelling in the center of Heaven and Earth. The cycles of **Yin and Yang** <term>the complementary forces of dark/light or passive/active</term>, the changes of heat and cold, and the transition of day and night are all determined by Heaven; Humanity simply listens and does not interfere with these natural laws. 

Though this one stalk "does not interfere," it is always present and used in conjunction with the other forty-eight stalks. Together, they form the **Three Powers** <term>sancai; the triad of Heaven, Earth, and Humanity</term>. Next, use the right hand to count the stalks held in the left hand by groups of four, and set aside the remainder...

It explains how the remaining forty-eight stalks mathematically correspond to the structure of the Eight Trigrams and their transformations.</summary>

<keywords>yarrow stalks, Two Powers, Three Powers, divination ritual, counting method, Heaven Earth and Humanity, trigrams, hexagram lines</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical description of the yarrow stalk divination process (the "counting method"). ### Illustrated Explanation of the Images of Change: Inner Chapters

Place one, two, three, or four [stalks] between the pinky and the ring finger; this is called the **remainder** <term>le; the stalks left over after counting the main pile by fours</term>. This symbolizes the **leap month** <note>original: "閏" (rùn)</note> that occurs once every three years. 

Next, use the right hand to take the stalks from the right half. If the remainder is one, take three; if the remainder is three, take one; if the remainder is two, take six; if the remainder is four, take four. Place these between the ring finger and the middle finger; this is called the **second remainder**. This symbolizes the rule of **two leap months** occurring within a five-year period. 

Afterward, "hanging" refers to taking the total of these two groups of remainders <note>the "odd" stalks</note> and placing them together with the single stalk that was originally "hung" <note>the stalk representing Humanity</note>, following the standard "hanging one" method. The *Yunhui* <note>The *Guangyun Yunhui Juyao*, a famous 13th-century phonological dictionary and encyclopedia</note> says: "Combine them and set them aside." 

The number of stalks in this combined remainder will always be either four or eight <gloss>there is no such thing as it being five or nine</gloss>. Obtaining four is considered "odd" <gloss>representing one unit of four</gloss>; obtaining eight is considered "even" <gloss>representing two units of four</gloss>. 

At this point, recombine the stalks that were counted through—which will now number either forty-four or forty. Divide, count, and "hang" them according to the previous method to perform the **Second Transformation** <term>er bian; the second of three ritual steps required to determine a single line of a hexagram</term>. Then, combine the remaining stalks from that counting—which will now number forty, thirty-six, or...

<vocab>揲蓍 (shéshī - counting yarrow stalks), 扐 (lè - remainder between fingers), 策 (cè - stalks/tallies), 二變 (èr biàn - second transformation), 易象 (yìxiàng - images of the Changes), 韻會 (Yùnhuì - the Yunhui dictionary)</vocab>

<meta>This page continues the technical description of the yarrow stalk divination process.
