### History of Jin: Complete Library of the Four Treasuries, Volume 2

[Count the remaining] thirty-two stalks, then divide, return, and "hang" them <note>the process of counting by fours and setting aside the remainder stalks between the fingers</note> according to the previous method; this constitutes the **third change**. After these three changes are complete, one then inspects the stalks that have been "hung." 

If one obtains three "odd" results <gloss>referring to the sets of remainder stalks from the three rounds of counting</gloss>, this is called **Old Yang** <term>laoyang; a changing yang line, numerically represented by nine</term>. If one obtains three "even" results, this is called **Old Yin** <term>laoyin; a changing yin line, numerically represented by six</term>. 

Where there are two evens and one odd, the odd serves as the master, and this is called **Young Yang** <term>shaoyang; a stable yang line, numerically represented by seven</term>. Where there are two odds and one even, the even serves as the master, and this is called **Young Yin** <term>shaoyin; a stable yin line, numerically represented by eight</term>. 

Every three changes <note>three full rounds of the counting process</note> forms a single **line** <term>yao; one of the six horizontal lines that make up a hexagram</term>. Eighteen changes are required to form one full **hexagram** <term>gua</term>. One hexagram can transform into any of the **sixty-four hexagrams**, and within those, all **four thousand and ninety-six** possible configurations are contained <note>this calculation represents the 64 hexagrams multiplied by 64 potential transformations for each (64 x 64 = 4096)</note>.

It details the numerological logic used to determine whether a line is "old" (changing) or "young" (stable).</summary>

<note>This diagram illustrates the "counting by fours" (modulo 4) logic used in I Ching divination. When the bundle of stalks is divided into two piles (representing Heaven and Earth), the remainders of each pile after dividing by four will always complement each other to form specific sets.</note>

| Left: 2 dots | Right: 2 dots | Left: 3 dots | Right: 1 dot |
| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| **Left: 4 dots** | **Right: 4 dots** | **Left: 1 dot** | **Right: 3 dots** |

->Left  Right  Left  Right<-

The Commentary <note>The *Xici Zhuan* or "Great Commentary" of the *I Ching*</note> says: "Sort them by four to represent the four seasons. Return the remainders to the opening between the fingers <term>le; the physical act of tucking remainder stalks between the fingers</term> to represent the intercalary month. In five years there are two intercalary months; therefore, one performs the 'returning' twice and then 'hangs' the stalks."

This diagram illustrates the remainders left over after sorting by four. If the left hand has a remainder of **one**, then the right hand must have a remainder of **three**. If the left hand has a remainder of **three**, then the right hand must have a remainder of **one**. These are the **Yang numbers** of the "returned remainders" of the **Two Modes** <term>liangyi; the primary forces of Yin and Yang</term>.

If the left hand has a remainder of **two**, then the right hand also has a remainder of **two**. If the left hand has a remainder of **four**, then the right hand also has a remainder of **four**. These are the **Yin numbers** of the "returned remainders" of the **Two Modes**.

It classifies the resulting remainders into Yin and Yang categories based on whether they are even or odd pairings.</summary>

# Imperial Commissioned Complete Library of the Four Treasuries, Volume II

Master Zhu <note>Zhu Xi (1130–1200), the most influential philosopher of the Neo-Confucian tradition</note> says: The image of the **Odd** <note>original: "竒" (*ji*); refers to the undivided Yang line</note> is circular. A circle with a diameter of one has a circumference of three; thus, **Yang** uses its full value. **Old Yang** consists of three **Odd** components: three times three equals **nine**. The number of stalks remaining after sorting <term>guodie; the final count of stalks in a divination step after removing the remainders</term> is likewise four times nine, which is **thirty-six**.

The image of the **Even** <note>original: "偶" (*ou*); refers to the divided Yin line</note> is square. A square with a diameter <note>width</note> of one has a perimeter of four; **Yin** uses half of its value <note>Using half of the perimeter (2) to contrast with the circle’s 3</note>. **Old Yin** consists of three **Even** components: three times two equals **six**. The number of stalks remaining after sorting is likewise four times six, which is **twenty-four**.

**Young Yang** consists of one **Odd** and two **Even** components. Summing one **3** and two **2s** equals **seven**. The number of stalks remaining after sorting is likewise four times seven, which is **twenty-eight**.

**Young Yin** consists of one **Even** and two **Odd** components. Summing one **2** and two **3s** equals **eight**. The number of stalks remaining after sorting is likewise four times eight, which is **thirty-two**.

This is the natural wonder of the numbers derived from the yarrow stalks <term>shishu; the mathematical results of the divination process</term> and the drawing of the hexagram lines <term>guahua; the physical representation of the results as Yin or Yang lines</term>.

<summary>This page records Zhu Xi’s geometric justification for the numbers 6, 7, 8, and 9 used in I Ching divination, linking circular and square properties to the final counts of yarrow stalks.</summary>
<keywords>Zhu Xi, Old Yang, Old Yin, Young Yang, Young Yin, yarrow stalk divination, sacred geometry, circle and square, I Ching mathematics</keywords>

# Explanatory Diagrams of the Changes’ Imagery: Inner Chapters

To the right <note>meaning the preceding charts and text</note>, every three changes <note>the three-step process of sorting yarrow stalks to determine a single line</note> forms one line <term>yao; the individual horizontal lines that compose a hexagram</term>. 

If all three changes result in odd numbers, it is categorized as Heaven <term>Qian; the trigram representing pure creative energy</term>. Its mark is <insert>□</insert> <note>symbolizing 'Old Yang'</note>, identifying it as Yang changing into Yin. 

If all three changes result in even numbers, it is categorized as Earth <term>Kun; the trigram representing pure receptive energy</term>. Its mark is <insert>✕</insert> <note>symbolizing 'Old Yin'</note>, identifying it as Yin changing into Yang. 

If the three changes result in two odds and one even, the even number is the master <note>it determines the line's nature</note>. Whether that even number occurs in the first, second, or third position, it is categorized as Wind <term>Xun</term>, Fire <term>Li</term>, or Lake <term>Dui</term>. Its mark is <insert>⚋</insert> <note>a 'Young Yin' line</note> and it does not change. 

If the three changes result in two evens and one odd, the odd number is the master. Whether that odd number occurs in the first, second, or third position, it is categorized as Thunder <term>Zhen</term>, Water <term>Kan</term>, or Mountain <term>Gen</term>. Its mark is <insert>⚊</insert> <note>a 'Young Yang' line</note> and it does not change. 

Altogether, there are these eight forms, which are the images of the Eight Trigrams <term>Bagua</term>. <gloss>There are twenty-four forms for the solid line <note>⚊</note>, twenty-four forms for the broken line <note>⚋</note>, eight forms for the square <note>□</note>, and eight forms for the cross <note>✕</note>, totaling</gloss> sixty-four.

The diagram's explanation follows below.

<summary>This page explains the mathematical results of yarrow stalk divination, specifically how "three changes" produce either stable lines (Young Yin/Yang) or changing lines (Old Yin/Yang). It categorizes these results into the Eight Trigrams and accounts for the 64 possible outcomes of the divination process.</summary>
<keywords>I Ching, Eight Trigrams, divination, yarrow stalks, Old Yang, Old Yin, Young Yang, Young Yin, hexagram lines, transformation</keywords>

# Inner Chapters of the Illustrations and Explanations of the Diagrams of the Changes

To the right <note>Referring to the diagrams presented on the previous page</note> are the configurations of two even lines and one odd line. With the odd line as the primary element, these constitute the tallies <term>ce (策); the individual yarrow stalks used in the divination process</term> for the **Young Yang** <note>Non-changing Yang lines</note> trigrams: **Mountain** <term>Gen (艮)</term>, **Water** <term>Kan (坎)</term>, and **Thunder** <term>Zhen (震)</term>. There are twenty-four in total.

Obtaining the odd line at the third change results in the eight tallies for **Mountain**.
Obtaining the odd line at the second change results in the eight tallies for **Water**.
Obtaining the odd line at the first change results in the eight tallies for **Thunder**.

The stalks set aside <term>guile (歸扐); the remainder stalks placed between the fingers during the sorting process</term> total twenty tallies. The stalks counted out <term>guodie (過揲); the active stalks counted in sets of four to determine the ritual result</term> total twenty-eight tallies. 

When dividing these by four:
The stalks set aside yield five <gloss>five units</gloss> <gloss>of four</gloss>.
The stalks counted out yield seven <gloss>seven units</gloss> <gloss>of four</gloss>.
This result constitutes the "Correct Yang," which is stable and does not change <note>In I Ching divination, a sum of 7 represents a "Young Yang" line. Unlike an "Old Yang" (9), a Young Yang line is considered stable and does not transform into its opposite (Yin) during the reading</note>.

<summary>This page provides the mathematical verification for the "Young Yang" trigrams (Mountain, Water, and Thunder), explaining how specific sorting results lead to a total of 28 stalks, which reduces to the number seven. This number seven identifies a stable Yang line that does not undergo transformation during divination.</summary>

### Inner Chapters of the Illustrations and Explanations of the Diagrams of the Changes

To the right <note>This refers to the diagrammatic layouts on the preceding pages of the manuscript.</note> are the cases of two odd and one even [results], where the even is dominant. These constitute the tallies for **Young Yin** <term>Shao Yin (少陰); the stable or "young" form of the feminine/receptive force, which is numerically represented by the number 8.</term> in the trigrams of **Wind**, **Fire**, and **Lake**. These tallies total twenty-four.

Where the even result is obtained during the first change <note>The first of three specific manipulations of the yarrow stalks required to form a single line of a hexagram.</note>, there are eight tallies for **Wind** <term>Xun (巽); the trigram representing Wind or Wood.</term>. Where the even is obtained during the second change, there are eight tallies for **Fire** <term>Li (離); the trigram representing Fire or Radiance.</term>. Where the even is obtained during the third change, there are eight tallies for **Lake** <term>Dui (兌); the trigram representing the Lake or Marsh.</term>. 

The discarded stalks <term>Gui she (歸揲); the yarrow stalks set aside after the division process.</term> total sixteen tallies. The remaining stalks <term>Guo she (過揲); the stalks kept and counted to determine the value of the hexagram line.</term> total thirty-two tallies. 

When divided by four: the discarded stalks result in four <gloss>four units</gloss>, and the remaining stalks result in eight <gloss>eight units</gloss>. This constitutes the **Correct Yin**, which does not change <note>In I Ching divination, a "young" line (7 or 8) is considered stable and does not transform into its opposite, unlike "old" or "moving" lines (6 or 9).</note>.

<summary>This page provides the mathematical summation for "Young Yin" lines, explaining how the manipulation of yarrow stalks results in the number eight for the trigrams of Wind, Fire, and Lake. It confirms these lines are stable and do not undergo transformation during divination.</summary>

To the right, the three changes are all even, forming the "Old Yin." The stalks for the hexagram Earth <term>Kun; the hexagram representing the Receptive principle and the Earth</term> consist of eight. Returning the remainders yields twenty-four stalks <term>ce; the individual yarrow stalks used in the ritual</term>, and the counting of the bundles <term>guo she; the process of counting out stalks by fours</term> also yields twenty-four stalks. By dividing these by four, the remainder bundles result in six <gloss>six units</gloss> and the counted bundles also result in six <gloss>six units</gloss>. This "four times six" is the numerical extreme of Yin, which then transforms into Yang. The *Book of Changes* uses these transformations to determine the divination; thus, all Yin lines that undergo change are referred to as "Six."

To the right, the eight basic trigrams each have eight variations, which combine to form the images of the sixty-four hexagrams. The counting method <term>she fa; the specific manual technique for manipulating the stalks</term> follows the teachings of Master Cheng <note>Cheng Yi, 1033–1107, a foundational Neo-Confucian philosopher</note> and Master Zhang <note>Zhang Zai, 1020–1077, known for his work on cosmology</note>. They stated that during the second and third changes, one does not "hang" <term>gua; the act of placing a single stalk between the fingers to represent the human element</term> a stalk to obtain the result. This is because, once a single stalk has been "hung" during the first change to symbolize Humanity and then set aside, the subsequent two changes follow that lead and do not require another hanging. In counting the piles, one counts the left pile and not the right, following the two schools of Zhang Yuan <gloss>and</gloss> Zhuang Chuo from the Tang Dynasty. This is based on the principle that Heaven is in motion while Earth is still, and that Yang...

The transformation of Yin matches the Earth. It follows the movement of Heaven; in terms of its underlying significance, this is appropriate. When the left hand has a remainder of one and the right hand matches it with three; or when the left remains three and is matched by one—this constitutes the "odd" number of Yang. When the left hand has a remainder of two and the right hand matches it with six; or when the left remains four and is matched by four—this constitutes the "even" number of Yin.

In this way, the "Greater" and "Lesser" <note>Also referred to as "Old" and "Young"</note> forms of Yin and Yang are kept uniform, balanced, and correct. However, if one follows the modern practice where a stalk is "hung" <term>gua; the act of placing one yarrow stalk between the fingers to represent the human element</term> during all three transformations, and both the left and right hands are counted through <term>she; the process of counting out stalks by fours</term>, the resulting probabilities would be: twelve for Old Yang, twenty-eight for Young Yin, twenty for Young Yang, and four for Old Yin. Such results are irregular, and the distribution of "many" versus "few" is inconsistent.

Furthermore, if one "hangs" a stalk during the first transformation but does not "hang" one during the subsequent two transformations, while still counting through both the left and right hands, the results would be: twenty-seven for Old Yang <gloss>Greater Yang</gloss>, twenty-seven for Young Yin, nine for Young Yang, and only one for Old Yin <gloss>Greater Yin</gloss>. Although these totals still reach sixty-four <note>The total number of possible outcomes for a hexagram line in these systems</note>, the manifestation of the Old Yin is extremely rare. This is because there is ultimately...

### Volume Lower
<margin>Gold Cave Four [...] Volume Lower</margin>

The stalks for **Pure Yang** <term>Qian; the hexagram representing Heaven</term>, calculated by multiplying the number of **Old Yang** <note>the numerical value of a "changing" yang line is 36</note> (thirty-six) by the number of the six lines, total two hundred and sixteen. The stalks for **Pure Yin** <term>Kun; the hexagram representing Earth</term>, calculated by multiplying the number of **Old Yin** <note>the numerical value of a "changing" yin line is 24</note> (twenty-four) by the number of the six lines, total one hundred and forty-four. Combined, these total three hundred and sixty, which corresponds to the days of a complete cycle <note>a ritual or symbolic year</note>. 

A cycle of twelve months constitutes one year. If we speak in terms of the **New Moon** <term>shuo; the lunar month</term>, there are three hundred and fifty-four days. If we speak in terms of the **Solar Terms** <term>qi; the solar year</term>, there are three hundred and sixty-five and one-quarter days. By taking the middle number between the **Solar Excess** <term>ying; the days by which the solar year exceeds 360</term> and the **Lunar Deficiency** <term>xu; the days by which the lunar year falls short of 360</term>, we arrive at the figure of three hundred and sixty. 

However, the stalks for **Young Yang** <note>the stable, non-changing yang line</note> number twenty-eight; accumulating these for the six lines of **Pure Yang** results in one hundred and sixty-eight. The stalks for **Young Yin** <note>the stable, non-changing yin line</note> number thirty-two; accumulating these for the six lines of **Pure Yin** results in one hundred and ninety-two. Combined, these also total three... <note>The text likely continues to show that 168 + 192 also equals 360, proving the mathematical consistency of the system regardless of whether the lines are "Old" or "Young."</note>

# Imperial Collection of the Four Treasuries
## Inner Chapters of the Illustrated Explanation of the Symbols of the Changes

->Page Twenty-Three<-

The reason the **Great Commentary** <term>Dazhuan; the core philosophical commentary on the I Ching</term> does not mention the number one hundred and sixty is that the *Changes* utilizes the numbers Nine and Six <note>these numbers represent the "changing" or "old" states of Yang and Yin</note> and does not utilize Seven and Eight <note>these represent the "stable" or "young" states</note>. 

The "two volumes" refer to the sixty-four hexagrams contained in the Upper and Lower Classics. In total, there are 192 **Yang lines** <term>the solid lines (—) across all 64 hexagrams</term>. By multiplying these by the numerical value of **Old Yang** (thirty-six), the product is six thousand nine hundred and twelve. There are also 192 **Yin lines** <term>the broken lines (- -) across all 64 hexagrams</term>. By multiplying these by the numerical value of **Old Yin** (twenty-four), the product is four thousand six hundred and eight. Combined, these sums reach eleven thousand five hundred and twenty, which corresponds exactly to the **Number of All Things** <note>in Chinese cosmology, 11,520 is the symbolic sum of all distinct entities or phenomena in the universe</note>.

However, if one multiplies the 192 Yang lines by the value of **Young Yang** (twenty-eight) the product is five thousand three hundred and seventy-six. If one multiplies the 192 Yin lines by the value of **Young Yin** (thirty-two), the product is six thousand one hundred and forty-four. Combined, these also total eleven thousand five hundred and twenty. From this, we know that the values for Young Yin and Young Yang are hidden within the values of Old Yin and Old <note>the text cuts off here, likely continuing to explain the mathematical symmetry between changing and stable lines</note>.

<summary>This page explains the numerology of the I Ching, demonstrating how the total numerical value of all lines (both changing and stable) consistently equals 11,520, the symbolic "Number of All Things."</summary>
<keywords>Old Yang, Old Yin, Young Yang, Young Yin, Number of All Things, Great Commentary, numerology, hexagram lines</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the numerical properties of the I Ching, moving from the mathematical totals of all lines to the specific application of the numbers Nine and Six in divination. ### Volume Two

Among the Yang numbers, Seven and Nine are both Yang and odd; among the Yin numbers, Six and Eight are both Yin and even. This explains the purpose behind using Nine and Six for the hexagrams **Qian** and **Kun** <note>original: "九六之旨" (the purpose of 9 and 6); in the I Ching system, Nine represents 'Old Yang' and Six represents 'Old Yin.' These are the active numbers that signal a line is about to change into its opposite</note>.

**Hexagram Qian** <term>The Creative; composed of six solid lines</term>, "Use of Nine": A flight of dragons appears without a head. Good fortune.
**Hexagram Kun** <term>The Receptive; composed of six broken lines</term>, "Use of Six": Beneficial to persist in perpetual constancy.

The above clarifies the general rules for the changing numbers Nine and Six used in divination across all sixty-four hexagrams. Master **Zhu Xi** <note>1130–1200 AD; the most influential philosopher of the Neo-Confucian tradition</note> stated: "In any hexagram where all six lines are 'unchanging' <note>meaning the lines are "stable" Sevens or Eights rather than "changing" Nines or Sixes</note>, one should consult the **Judgment** <term>Tuan; the core oracular text of the hexagram</term>. In such cases, use the inner trigram as the *zhen* <term>the 'base' or 'subject' of the inquiry</term> and the outer trigram as the *hui* <term>the 'outer' or 'object' of the inquiry</term>."

According to the **Zuo Commentary** <term>Zuo Zhuan; an ancient historical record of the Spring and Autumn period</term>, Kong Chengzi once performed a divination regarding the installation of Prince Yuan of the State of Wei. He encountered Hexagram **Tun** <gloss>Thunder below, Water above</gloss> [Hexagram symbol ䷂]. The oracle said: "It is beneficial to establish a feudal lord."

When the Earl of Qin attacked the State of Jin, he performed a divination and encountered Hexagram **Gu** [Hexagram symbol ䷑] <gloss>Wind below, Mountain above</gloss>. It was said: "The *zhen* <note>the inner part</note> of Gu is Wind; its *hui* <note>the outer part</note> is Mountain." These are examples of divinations where the six lines do not change, and the interpretation relies on the hexagram's Judgment and the symbolic images of its component parts.

<summary>This page explains the fundamental role of the numbers Nine and Six in I Ching divination and provides the standard rules for interpreting hexagrams that contain no changing lines. It cites historical examples from the Zuo Commentary to illustrate how the inner and outer trigrams are used to interpret a fixed omen.</summary>

<keywords>Qian, Kun, Nine and Six, Zhu Xi, Zuo Commentary, Hexagram Tun, Hexagram Gu, Zhen and Hui, divination rules, inner and outer trigrams</keywords>

--- Page 141 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for I Ching divination, specifically moving from situations where no lines change to the rules for when one or two lines change. It cites famous historical divinations recorded in the Zuo Commentary (Zuo Zhuan) to illustrate these rules.</meta>

# Explanatory Illustrations of the Diagrams of Change: Inner Volume
<meta>By Imperial Decree: The Complete Library of the Four Treasuries</meta>

When **one line changes**, one performs the divination using the line text <term>yao ci; the specific poetic oracular text attached to one of the six lines of a hexagram</term> of the changing line in the original hexagram. 

**Example 1:** Bi Wan of the State of Jin <note>A high official in the 7th century BC whose descendants eventually founded the State of Wei</note> encountered Hexagram **Tun** (Difficulty at the Beginning).
<column-break/>
**Hexagram Tun (屯):** ☵ Water above, ☳ Thunder below
<gloss>Thunder below, Water above</gloss>
...which changed into Hexagram **Bi** (Holding Together).
**Hexagram Bi (比):** ☵ Water above, ☷ Earth below
<gloss>Earth below, Water above</gloss>
The change occurred at the **first line**, which was a Nine <note>a "Nine" is an "Old Yang" line that is active and about to change</note>.

**Example 2:** Cai Mo <note>A famous historian and diviner of the State of Jin</note> encountered Hexagram **Qian** (The Creative).
<column-break/>
**Hexagram Qian (乾):** ☰ Heaven above, ☰ Heaven below
<gloss>Heaven below, Heaven above</gloss>
...which changed into Hexagram **Tongren** (Fellowship with Men).
**Hexagram Tongren (同人):** ☰ Heaven above, ☲ Fire below
<gloss>Fire below, Heaven above</gloss>
The change occurred at the **second line**, which was a Nine.

**Example 3:** Duke Wen of Jin <note>One of the "Five Hegemons" of the Spring and Autumn period</note> encountered Hexagram **Dayou** (Possession in Great Measure).
<column-break/>
**Hexagram Dayou (大有):** ☲ Fire above, ☰ Heaven below
<gloss>Heaven below, Fire above</gloss>
...which changed into Hexagram **Kui** (Opposition).
**Hexagram Kui (睽):** ☲ Fire above, ☱ Lake below
<gloss>Lake below, Fire above</gloss>
The change occurred at the **third line**, which was a Nine.

**Example 4:** Chen Jingzhong <note>Also known as Tian Wan, a prince who fled to the State of Qi</note> encountered Hexagram **Guan** (Contemplation).
<column-break/>
**Hexagram Guan (觀):** ☴ Wind above, ☷ Earth below
<gloss>Earth below, Wind above</gloss>
...which changed into Hexagram **Pi** (Standstill).
**Hexagram Pi (否):** ☰ Heaven above, ☷ Earth below
<gloss>Earth below, Heaven above</gloss>
The change occurred at the **fourth line**, which was a Six <note>a "Six" is an "Old Yin" line that is active and about to change</note>.

**Example 5:** Nan Kuai <note>An official of the State of Lu who planned a rebellion</note> encountered Hexagram **Kun** (The Receptive).
<column-break/>
**Hexagram Kun (坤):** ☷ Earth above, ☷ Earth below
<gloss>Earth below, Earth above</gloss>
...which changed into Hexagram **Bi** (Holding Together).
**Hexagram Bi (比):** ☵ Water above, ☷ Earth below
<gloss>Earth below, Water above</gloss>
The change occurred at the **fifth line**, which was a Six.

**Example 6:** Duke Xian of Jin encountered Hexagram **Guimei** (The Marrying Maiden).
<column-break/>
**Hexagram Guimei (歸妹):** ☳ Thunder above, ☱ Lake below
<gloss>Lake below, Thunder above</gloss>
...which changed into Hexagram **Kui** (Opposition).
**Hexagram Kui (睽):** ☲ Fire above, ☱ Lake below
<gloss>Lake below, Fire above</gloss>
The change occurred at the **top line**, which was a Six. 

These cases illustrate the rule of using the original hexagram’s line text when **one line changes**.
<column-break/>
When **two lines change**, one uses the line texts of both changing lines from the original hexagram for the divination, though the upper changing line is considered the primary indicator. However, there are no recorded examples of this in the historical commentaries <note>Refers to the fact that the Zuo Zhuan does not contain a specific case study for a two-line change</note>.

<summary>This page explains the method for interpreting a divination when exactly one line of a hexagram changes, providing six historical examples from ancient Chinese records. It also briefly introduces the rule for two-line changes, noting a lack of historical precedents.</summary>
<keywords>divination rules, one line change, Zuo Zhuan, Bi Wan, Duke Wen of Jin, hexagram transformation, line text, yao ci</keywords>

--- Page 142 ---
<meta>This page continues the technical instructions for I Ching divination, moving from instances where one or two lines change to the more complex rules for when three or four lines change. It continues to use historical precedents from the Zuo Commentary to validate these methods.</meta>

<page-type>text</page-type>
<sig>Inner Volume</sig>

# Examples of Rules

When **three lines change**, one performs the divination using the Decision texts <term>Tuan Ci; the overall prophetic statement for a whole hexagram</term> of both the **Original Hexagram** and the **Resultant Hexagram**. The Original Hexagram is considered the "Inner" <term>Zhen; representing the subject or the beginning of the matter</term> and the Resultant Hexagram is the "Outer" <term>Hui; representing the object or the outcome</term>. 

**Example:** Prince Chong’er <note>the future Duke Wen of Jin, who lived in exile for nineteen years before returning to power</note> performed a divination regarding whether he would regain his kingdom. He encountered the "Inner" Hexagram **Tun** (Difficulty at the Beginning).
Hexagram Tun (屯): ☵ Water over ☳ Thunder
<gloss>Thunder below, Water above</gloss>
The "Outer" result was Hexagram **Yu** (Joy/Exuberance).
Hexagram Yu (豫): ☳ Thunder over ☷ Earth
<gloss>Earth below, Thunder above</gloss>

In this case, the lines were "all eights" <note>In divination, the numbers 7 and 8 represent stable lines that do not change, while 6 and 9 represent lines in flux</note>. This occurred because the first and fifth lines were Nines <note>Active Yang</note> and the fourth line was a Six <note>Active Yin</note>. Thus, a total of three lines changed. The second, third, and top lines remained "eights" in both hexagrams. The Minister of Works, Jizi, interpreted this, saying: "Both hexagrams indicate it is 'favorable to establish feudal lords.'" This is the standard example for when **three lines change**. 

Furthermore, if the change occurs within the first ten hexagrams of a sequence, the "Inner" (Zhen) governs the meaning; if it occurs in the latter ten hexagrams, the "Outer" (Hui) governs. 
<note>original: "見啓蒙六十四卦變圖" — See the "Chart of the Changes of the Sixty-Four Hexagrams" in the *Introduction to the Study of the I Ching* by the philosopher Chu Hsi.</note>

When **four lines change**, one performs the divination using the two lines that **did not change** in the Resultant Hexagram. Among these two, the lower line is still treated as the primary indicator. However, there are no surviving examples of this specific rule in the ancient Commentaries <note>referring to the historical records of the Zuo Zhuan</note>.

<summary>This page explains the technical rules for interpreting a divination when three or four lines of a hexagram change, using the famous historical case of Prince Chong'er to illustrate the three-line rule.</summary>
<keywords>three lines change, four lines change, Prince Chong'er, Hexagram Tun, Hexagram Yu, Zhen and Hui, Jizi, I Ching divination methodology</keywords>

The previous page covered cases where three or four lines change; this page concludes the sequence by explaining what to do when five or all six lines change, citing historical errors and classic precedents from the Zuo Commentary.</meta>

<page-type>text</page-type>
<sig>Illustrated Explanation of the Diagrams of the I Ching: Inner Volume</sig>

# Examples

When **five lines change**, one performs the divination using the single **unchanging line** of the Resultant Hexagram <term>Zhi Gua; the hexagram that one "goes to" after the lines have shifted</term>. For example, when Mu Jiang <note>A noblewoman of the State of Lu in the 6th century BCE, known for her involvement in political intrigue and her subsequent confinement</note> went to the East Palace, she encountered "Gen's Eight" <note>This is a famous historical riddle in the Zuo Commentary. It refers to a divination where the result was Hexagram Gen, but the interpretation focused on the specific line that did not change—the "eight"</note>.

Hexagram **Gen** (Mountain): ☶ over ☶
<gloss>Mountain below, Mountain above</gloss>
The scribe said: "This is what is called **Gen** changing to **Sui**."
Hexagram **Sui** (Following): ☱ over ☳
<gloss>Thunder below, Lake above</gloss>

In this case, the first, fourth, and fifth lines were "Sixes" <note>Changing Yin lines</note>, and the third and top lines were "Nines" <note>Changing Yang lines</note>. Thus, a total of five lines changed. Only the **second line** was an "Eight" <note>A stable Yin line that does not change</note>. 

According to the rules, one should have used the second line of the Resultant Hexagram, **Sui**, as the prophecy. That line reads: "Attached to the little boy, one loses the mature man." However, the scribe of that time falsely cited the general Decision text <term>Tuan Ci; the overall judgment for the entire hexagram</term> of the Sui hexagram to answer her. This was an error.

When **all six lines change**, for most hexagrams, one uses the Decision text of the **Resultant Hexagram** for the prophecy. The only exceptions are Hexagram **Qian** (The Creative/Heaven) and Hexagram **Kun** (The Receptive/Earth), which have their own specific "Universal" line readings <term>Yong Jiu and Yong Liu; special instructions used only when all lines of these two primary hexagrams change</term>. 

Cai Mo <note>An official and scholar of the State of Jin known for his expertise in the I Ching</note> once discussed **Qian** changing into **Kun**, saying: "A flight of dragons appears without a head—Auspicious." This is the standard example for this rule. In this way, a single hexagram can change in six...

<summary>This page explains the specific rules for complex divination results where five or six lines change, providing a critique of a historical misinterpretation involving Lady Mu Jiang. It also notes the unique rules for the primary hexagrams Qian and Kun when they undergo a total transformation.</summary>

<keywords>five lines change, six lines change, Mu Jiang, Hexagram Gen, Hexagram Sui, Cai Mo, Qian to Kun, divination rules, Zuo Commentary, Tuan Ci</keywords>

It then marks the formal beginning of the second volume of the "Inner Chapters."</meta>

->By Imperial Decree: Complete Library of the Four Treasuries<-

Within the **sixty-four hexagrams**, the **four thousand and ninety-six hexagrams** <note>This refers to the mathematical expansion of the I Ching: 64 multiplied by 64. It represents the total number of possible transitions when one hexagram changes into another</note> are already contained. By extending and stretching these meanings, and by connecting similar types to further expand them, all the potential tasks and affairs under heaven are encompassed and completed.

# Illustrated Explanation of the Diagrams of the *I Ching*: Inner Volume, Part Two

<term>Yi Xiang Tu Shuo; Illustrated Explanation of the Diagrams of the I Ching</term>
<term>Gua; Hexagram, the six-line symbols that form the core of the I Ching</term>
<term>Si Ku Quan Shu; The "Complete Library of the Four Treasuries," a massive 18th-century collection of Chinese literature and philosophy commissioned by the Qianlong Emperor</term>

<meta>This page serves as an editorial colophon or a list of textual corrections for the "Inner Chapters" of the Illustrated Explanation of the Diagrams of the I Ching. It details specific instances where the current editors found errors in previous printed versions—likely the Ming dynasty editions—and corrected them for the "Complete Library of the Four Treasuries" (Si Ku Quan Shu) edition. It follows the title page of the second volume.</meta>

I respectfully note that in the **Inner Chapters**, Volume One, Page 2, within the first five lines: "those who form the <term>Bagua; The Eight Trigrams, the fundamental symbols of the I Ching</term> consist of three positions." <note>original: "成八卦者三位也"</note>
The previous printed edition mistakenly recorded "three" where it should have been "two"; this has now been corrected.

On Page 2, within the first five lines in the lower section: "then six, eight, and ten constitute the three positions."
The previous printed edition mistakenly recorded "three" where it should have been "two"; this has now been corrected.

On Page 6, within the first five lines: "elucidating the meaning of <term>Heaven’s motion and Earth’s stillness; A core cosmological concept where the sky/cosmos is active (Yang) and the earth is receptive/still (Yin)</term>." <note>original: "發明天動地靜之義"</note>
The previous printed edition mistakenly used the character for "method" instead of "elucidate"; this has now been corrected.

On Page 11, within the first five lines: "received the <term>He Tu; The River Map, a mystical cosmogram said to have been revealed to the mythical sage Fuxi</term> principles and then drew them."
The previous printed edition omitted the character for "principles" <note>original: "則" (ze)</note>. This likely follows a known error found in the *Yi Xue Qi Meng* <note>The "Introduction to the Study of the I Ching" by the Song dynasty scholar Zhu Xi</note>; it has now been restored based on the text of the <term>Han Shu; The Book of Han, the classic history of the Han Dynasty</term>.

<summary>This page records specific textual corrections made by the Qing dynasty editors to resolve errors in earlier printed versions of the "Inner Chapters." The editors cross-referenced historical texts like the Book of Han to ensure the accuracy of phrases regarding the Eight Trigrams and the River Map.</summary>

<keywords>textual criticism, Bagua, He Tu, River Map, Book of Han, Yi Xue Qi Meng, errata, cosmology, Si Ku Quan Shu</keywords>

On Page 15, in the final line: "to grasp it with the <term>hand radical; The character component '彐' (ji), which depicts a hand reaching down to hold something</term> and calculate it." <note>original: "以彐持而筭之"</note>
In the previous printed edition, the character was mistakenly written as "again" <note>original: "又" (you)</note> instead of the hand radical; this has now been corrected.

The "Old Explanations" and other accompanying notes previously followed the <term>Temple printed edition; A version of the text produced by a Buddhist or Taoist temple, which served as a common source for scholars but often contained regional or clerical errors</term>. Since the temple edition was erroneous in these instances, they have now all been corrected.

# List of Corrections

### Middle Scroll, Page 5, bottom two lines:
"In ancient times, when **Pao Xi** <note>original: "包犠"</note> <term>Pao Xi; An archaic name for Fu Xi, the mythical first emperor of China and creator of the Eight Trigrams</term> ruled the world." In the previous printed edition, **Pao Xi** was written as **Fu Yi** <note>original: "伏義"</note>; this has now been corrected.

### Page 11, bottom six lines:
"The frame and **enclosure** <note>original: "廓" (kuo)</note> <term>enclosure; Here referring to the cosmic container or the "cauldron" used in alchemical transformation</term> of **Kan and Li** <note>original: "坎離"</note> <term>Kan and Li; The trigrams for Water and Fire, representing the fundamental polarities of Yin and Yang</term>." In the previous printed edition, **enclosure** <note>original: "廓"</note> was mistakenly written as **outer city wall** <note>original: "郭" (guo)</note>; this has been corrected based on the text of the **Cantong qi** <note>original: "參同契"</note> <term>Cantong qi; "The Kinship of the Three," a seminal 2nd-century text on Taoist internal alchemy</term>.

### Page 15, bottom line:
"The **Already and Not Yet Fulfilled** <note>original: "既未濟"</note> <term>Already and Not Yet Fulfilled; Referring to Hexagram 63 (Ji Ji) and Hexagram 64 (Wei Ji), signifying the completion of a cycle and the beginning of the next</term> states of Kan and Li." In the previous printed edition, the character **fulfilled** <note>original: "濟" (ji)</note> was missing; it has now been added.

### Page 18, bottom line:
"Moving and **setting upright** <note>original: "植" (zhi)</note>." In the previous printed edition, **setting upright** was erroneously written as **straight** <note>original: "直" (zhi)</note>; this has now been corrected.

### Page 19, top line:
"**Like** <note>original: "如" (ru)</note> the grinding of a stone." In the previous printed edition, the word **like** was erroneously written as **to know** <note>original: "知" (zhi)</note>; this has now been corrected.

### Page 23, top two lines:
"The **common people** <note>original: "黎" (li)</note> <term>common people; The term "Li" refers to the "black-haired people," a classical Chinese way of addressing the populace</term> obtain constancy." In the previous printed edition, **common people** was erroneously written as **goosefoot herb** <note>original: "藜" (li)</note>; this has been corrected according to the **Cantong qi** <note>original: "參同契"</note>...

# Corrections Based on the *Cantong qi*

### Page 23, bottom two lines:
"**Relinquishing** <note>original: "遜" (xun)</note> <term>relinquishing; In this context, to cede or step down from a position of power, often referring to a ruler or an aspirant to immortality leaving worldly life</term> the worldly position." In the previous printed edition, the word **relinquishing** was mistakenly written as **approaching** <note>original: "適" (shi)</note>. This has been corrected according to the **Cantong qi** <note>original: "參同契"</note>.

### Page 23, bottom four lines:
"Observe its **shining radiance** <note>original: "耀景" (yaojing)</note>." Note: In the **Collectanea edition** <note>original: "叢書本" (Congshu ben)</note> <term>Collectanea edition; A version of a text found in a large, multi-volume library of collected works</term> of the *Cantong qi*, this passage reads as "Observe its **weights and measures** <note>original: "權量" (quanliang)</note>," which differs from the present text.
<note>The "shining radiance" likely refers to the spiritual light produced during alchemical meditation, whereas "weights and measures" refers to the precise timing or "fire-phasing" used in the refining process.</note>

### Page 24, bottom six lines:
"Those things regarding **good and bad fortune, regret and remorse** <note>original: "吉凶悔吝者" (ji xiong hui lin zhe)</note>." In the previous printed edition, the character for **those** <note>original: "者" (zhe)</note> <term>those; A grammatical particle used here to nominalize the preceding phrase, essentially meaning "the matters of..."</term> was omitted. It has now been added.
<note>The phrase "good and bad fortune, regret and remorse" is a direct reference to the four types of prognostications found in the I Ching (Book of Changes), representing the fluctuations of fate.</note>

<keywords>Cantong qi, philology, textual emendation, I Ching, alchemical radiance, weights and measures, Taoist cultivation</keywords>

# Errata for the Lower Volume

### Volume Two, Page 4, Recto, Line 2:
"...and below, it **tends** <note>original: "趨" (qu)</note> toward the abdomen." In the previous printed edition, **tends** was mistakenly written as **originates** <note>original: "起" (qi)</note>. This has now been corrected.

### Page 16, Recto, Line 4:
"Five-**four** <note>original: "五箇四" (wu ge si)</note>." The printed edition mistakenly wrote "five-**five** <note>original: "五箇五" (wu ge wu)</note>." This has now been corrected.

### Page 17, Verso, Top Right:
"The **stalks** <note>original: "策" (ce)</note> <term>stalks; Refers to the yarrow or milfoil stalks used in I Ching divination to calculate the numerical values of the hexagram lines</term> of the **Dui trigram** <note>original: "兑" (Dui)</note> <term>Dui; One of the eight trigrams, symbolizing the Lake or the Joyous, often associated with the west and the element of metal in alchemy</term>." The printed edition mistakenly used the **Qian trigram** <note>original: "乾" (Qian)</note> <term>Qian; The trigram symbolizing Heaven or the Creative, representing pure Yang energy</term> instead of **Dui**. This has now been corrected.

### Page 24, Recto, Line 6:
In the annotation for the diagram of the **Marrying Maiden hexagram** <note>original: "歸妹卦" (Guimei gua)</note> <term>Marrying Maiden hexagram; The 54th hexagram of the I Ching, which describes the integration of different forces</term>, the text reads: "**Dui below and Zhen above** <note>original: "兑下震上" (Dui xia Zhen shang)</note> <term>Zhen; The trigram symbolizing Thunder or the Arousing</term>." The printed edition mistakenly wrote "**Dui above and Zhen below**." This has now been corrected.

### Page 24, Verso, Line 2:
"The **third line** <note>original: "三爻" (san yao)</note> <term>line; Hexagrams are composed of six 'yao' or lines; a 'changing' line indicates a shift from Yin to Yang or vice-versa</term> changes." The printed edition mistakenly wrote "**second line** <note>original: "二爻" (er yao)</note>." This has now been corrected.
